In practice, there is a need for more substantial changes than just an amendment to seek external advice. Firstly, the Roadmap sometimes also applies to voluntary care. That is strange, I think, because it is not the case in the Law on compulsory mental health care. And if such a willing patient enters the hospital, such a bed fence could also be placed without any problems. I therefore think it is wrong that, after transfer to a care institution, this would be challenged by declaring the decision-making procedure for involuntary care to apply. I therefore ask the Minister whether he would like to use the legislative amendment announced to delete Article 2 (2) of the Health and Force Act. My second proposal for simplification concerns limiting the application of the roadmap to those forms of involuntary care for which no professional directive has yet been developed by the field. An amendment has been tabled in the House of Representatives to regulate this, so that the Roadmap takes the function of a safety net and drastically reduces the administrative burden. This means that it only applies to involuntary care, which has not been the subject of a professional directive. Customisation thus takes precedence over a standard procedure and is connected to the practice of administering medication. After all, the step-by-step plan of the Care and Force Act does not need to be followed if medication is prescribed according to the professional guidelines. Also Article 8:5 of the Compulsory ggz Act provides that mandatory care is to be provided on the basis of directives. I therefore ask the Minister whether he is prepared to include the said amendment in his forthcoming legislative proposal. This would really reduce the administrative burden on healthcare workers. The purpose of such a punishment is to do justice to the law that has been violated, to satisfy what has been done wrong. ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u11	Mr. Chairman. Today we are faced with a joint treatment of as many as three legislative proposals, the Law on compulsory mental health care, the Law on care and compulsion and the Law on forensic care, with the starting point of harmonizing the legal status of persons who have to undergo forced care. 50PLUS wholeheartedly supports the cultural change to avoid as much coercion as possible and to apply it only as an ultimatum remedium. But cultural change in mental health care must also come from within. Legislation only provides support. Finding a balance between the importance of the patient or client and the importance of society is and will remain difficult, but in all cases it is necessary to strive for customisation and the starting point "no, unless" should be central to the legal framework of forced care. In addition, alternatives to prevent forced care should be considered as far as possible. Mr. Chairman. Our group is focusing today on the Health and Force Act. With the introduction of the Social Support Act 2015, the Wmo, self-reliance and efforts to stay at home for as long as possible for people with mental illness is a fact. People with a psychogeriatric disorder or intellectual disability often need care on various aspects of their lives. A view of healing or improvement is lacking and the need for care will often increase over the years. The Health and Force Act introduces an arrangement for the provision of care to this group of people, even if they do not agree to it in any case. This involuntary care is also applicable in home situations, but it is not yet known about which forms of outpatient care will be involved because this needs to be further regulated at AMvB. At the end of October 2014, Dagblad Trouw reported that research carried out by Maastricht University on home-living dementias revealed that no less than 79% received involuntary care, for example by administering food, fluid or medication, and 7% was limited in their physical freedom through bonding or confinement in the home. According to the letter of the Secretary of State of State of the United States of America dated 26 June 2015 on psychopharmaceuticals that its medicinal products are active against mental illness.................................................................................................................................................................. And a third of clients in disability care institutions received this type of medicine even for more than 10 years in 78% of cases. In short, the administration of psychopharmaceuticals without the explicit consent of the patients was until recently rule rather than exception and, as it appeared, was often caused by procedural embarrassment on the part of the caregivers. The introduction of the Roadmap into the Health and Force Act sets rules for the application of involuntary care. These apply both in an accommodation where the client stays voluntarily or involuntarily, and in a home situation. As early as 2008 the Council of State placed our comments on the position of the resident client receiving extramural care for exactly a decade. Mr. Chairman. Does the Minister recognise that the risk of legal embarrassment in the care provider or caregiver in a home situation is many times greater than in an inpatient institution? Does the Minister consider that including involuntary care in a roadmap or care plan could effectively prevent the risk of disproportionate coercion in a home situation? Our group is by no means convinced of this, certainly not in the light of the facts that have just been mentioned. Can the Minister explain why the form of involuntary outpatient care is not included in the law, but should be further regulated at AMvB? I come to the distinction between the Law on compulsory mental health care and the Law on care and compulsion. The government has indicated that the Wvggz and Wzd legislative proposals each have their own target group and, accordingly, a different regime in terms of implementation and legal protection. However, as indicated at the expert meeting of 16 May 2017, in practice this distinction between the different target groups is not so clear. Often these are people with multi-problems. On the question of when which law is applicable, the Government states in the reply that it is up to a competent doctor to determine which disorder is dominant and that the concern for both legislative proposals is almost identical. But, Mr President, how can this answer be consistent with the essential difference between an inclusion under the Wvggz, which is always accompanied by a judicial review, and an inclusion under the Wzd, where the independent review is lacking if the client does not show willingness to but does not oppose admission? After all, according to Article 22 Act care and coercion is only required when the client opposes inclusion, a judicial authorisation is required. But in all cases this is a measure of freedom-limiting. Mr. Chairman. Especially in the case of a passive attitude of a client with a variety of disorders, it will be difficult to understand when the client actually resists or does not oppose admission. People suffering from mental illness in combination with dementia or mental disability often do not see the consequences of such inclusion in accommodation. For both categories such a recording is not temporary but rather permanent. Therefore, from the point of view of legal certainty and the desire to harmonise regulations, 50PLUS is in favour of the need for a judicial authorisation for admission to accommodation even in the case of a passive attitude by the client. How does the Minister feel about this? I come to the relationship between the healthcare provider and the Wzd doctor. The majority of the House of Representatives has rightly opted for the introduction of a Wzd doctor. However, Mr President, this raises questions in the field about the relationship between the care provider and the Wzd doctor and the concrete demarcation of powers. Each of them has independent powers. Both are responsible for the care plan in which involuntary care is included. In particular, the introduction of the Wzd doctor as final responsibility for the category of people with intellectual disabilities who do not need medical treatment is highly critical. The extra link of the Wzd doctor would have no added value for good care. My group agrees with one of the Minister's proposals made in a letter dated 18 December 2017 to amend the Health and Force Act in such a way that an EZ psychologist or an academicly trained orthopedagogist can also fulfil the role of Wzd-doctor, but he points out that the primacy for these three changes is, of course, with the Second Chamber. Nor does it make it easier to understand the already very complex matter. We wonder whether this is not a pre-announcement of many more future amendments to the apparently incomplete three legislative proposals. Mr. Chairman. If the present legislative proposals are to be adopted by our Chamber soon, the legislative changes already announced will have to be dealt with separately and the AMvBs will still have to be completed, what will this mean in the interim for the feasibility of this regulation? I'd like a response from the Minister. Let me now turn to the role of the district attorney and the medical statement. 50PLUS strongly supports the prevention of escalation by timely intervention. Who doesn't, I'd say. The role assigned to the family can contribute to this. After all, the family is often the first to receive signals about a deterioration in the psychic condition of a family member. If forced care appears inevitable, the Public Prosecutor may apply to the court for authorisation to take care, thereby forcing compulsory admission. This request must be accompanied by a medical certificate from a psychiatrist to identify the mental disorder and the causal relationship between the mental disorder and the serious disadvantage. Does this mean that the district attorney always takes the medical statement as an undisputed starting point? Will a request for authorisation for care be omitted if there is no causal link between the disorder and serious disadvantage? What vision prevails when the prosecutor disagrees with the medical certificate issued? In that case, can the district attorney obtain a second opinion on his own? Will the Public Prosecutor not focus primarily on preventing crime and therefore perhaps not be more inclined to assume the possibility of serious harm in advance? I'd like an explanation from the Minister. Finally, I have a question about forensic care. Our group would like to hear the Minister's response to the alternative proposal of the Dutch Data Protection Authority as set out in the letter of 11 January 2018 to create the possibility for the judges to impose a TBs measure within two years of the conviction becoming irrevocable. Is the Minister prepared to amend the law to this effect? Thank you.

When it was started to write the present laws, that was certainly imaginable, but in the course of time insights change. The law evaluation of ZonMw's forced care from 2014 is really different. The evaluation of twelve years later shows precisely that, for example, in the case of coercive medication, it is not clear why patients in the Wvggz have a different level of legal protection than those covered by the Health and Force Act. Why is it not better to see how this can be brought to a more equal level? Can the Minister explain this? It is also not easy to keep a clear and clear legislative proposal in a procedure of many years. We can see that, too. I suspect I may be the only senator who has applied coercive care to people with mental illness under the law of the Insanity Act and the Law special inclusion in psychiatric hospitals in practice. What if I'm willing and physically severely ill, but I refuse my treatment, which threatens an acute danger of life? That's the kind of trade-off I'm allowed to make. There'll be no grounds for coercive care. But if I'm willing and mentally ill and therefore no longer want to live, I don't think I should refuse this treatment. After the start there will be a lot more to repair. I therefore call for an appropriate two-year phasing-in period and for the possibility for professional groups and client organisations to provide feedback directly from the introduction to the VWS Ministry. I think it's kind of annoying, but you could call it a "colliding point." I would like to hear a response from the Minister on this matter. I'm coming to a closure. It is good to say that we will not now respond to the repair legislation announced by the Minister. The substantive discussion of these two subjects is initially a matter for the House of Representatives. The present legislative proposals focus on the care needs of the client, whether at home or in an institution. These are three legislative proposals which are coordinated but each has its own regime. However, this also means that there are differences between the Care and Force Act and the Compulsory ggz Act, for example in the judicial review of compulsory care. This has been discussed extensively in the debates in the House of Representatives. In response to this inequality, among other things, the Wzd doctor has been introduced by my party. The introduction of the previously appointed client confidant, by analogy with the patient confidant in the ggz, also has to do with this. It appears that the maximum degree of coordination has been achieved at present, knowing that this difference in the legal status of clients remains. As a SP, we are already looking forward to the forthcoming evaluation of the legislative proposals before us. The government has not chosen to design a completely new law on compulsory care. These three legislative proposals, each with their own origins, will have to be coordinated and properly implemented. All this in the context of a strong social discussion with opinions and views on confused persons, together with a ggz system that begins to crack down, among other things, through health insurance bureaucracy, the lack of psychiatrists and growing waiting lists. There are also problems in the care of the elderly, including staffing problems. This is not a good idea to roll out these three complex laws. As a SP Group, we agree with the basic principles of these legislative proposals, but our great concern lies in their future implementation. I ask the directors if they share this concern and if so, what they intend to do with it. Thank you.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u16	Thank you, Mr. Don. I give the floor to Mrs Wezel.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u17	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. First, I welcome the Ministers and the Secretary of State. I wish them much strength, wisdom and perseverance in order to carry out their important work. I will speak here on behalf of the SP Group and the Party for Animals Group on the Forensic Care Act. With this proposal, the prosecutor and the judge will have the opportunity to provide mental care to someone within criminal law more quickly. The main objectives of this bill are: the right patient in the right place, the creation of sufficient forensic care capacity, quality care focused on the safety of society and a good connection between forensic and curative care. By providing good care as part of the punishment, the cabinet wants to reduce the repetition of criminal offences. The SP and the Animal Party fully support the aforementioned objectives of the law and wish ministers to achieve these objectives. The SP and the Party for Animals consider it important that the condemned are punished for their crimes, but also that they are given such care that they return to society well prepared after their detention. The SP and the Party for Animals do see points of interest for the success of the bill: firstly, on the available funds for forensic care; secondly, on the link between the failing ggz system and the influx into forensic psychiatry; thirdly, on the subject of suspects who do not wish to participate in a Tbs observation; fourthly, on the action of the Health and Youth Inspectorate; and fifth and last in the matter of post-care. On the first point, I would like to ask whether the resources available are sufficient to achieve the objectives pursued. Given the news coverage, there is still a long way to go to achieve the aforementioned goals, for example in forensic youth care. The Netherlands Institute of Forensic Psychiatry and Psychology has an annual report on the minor suspect in about 700 youth, including an opinion to the judge on appropriate therapy to prevent the recurrence of the crime. Think of trainings that combat aggression or drug use, or family counseling and treatments in forensic mental health care. The judge often follows this advice. Research shows that young people receiving such treatments are much less likely to go over the line again. Young people who are not in therapy are at greater risk of committing another violent crime. If the judge is imposing therapy, it's obviously necessary, and then it has to be done. The District Attorney says the municipalities have to take care of it. Research carried out by the Volkskrant in August 2017 shows that young people who have been required to receive therapy often have to wait a long time in the criminal justice framework, sometimes for a year. This is due to waiting lists and the reluctance of municipalities, which have been responsible for special care for young people since decentralisation. According to the VNG, the juvenile court is not always well aware of the health care institutions with which municipalities have contracts and of any waiting lists. The UNG therefore talks with the judiciary to find out what youth assistance is needed and to give justice a better insight into which care has been purchased by municipalities. The problem lies more in the complexity of the new youth aid system than in the bureaucracy of municipalities, says a VNG spokesperson. A spokesperson of the youth psychiatric center de Bascule in Amsterdam reported in a newspaper report that the sharp cuts in the specialist youth aid reduced the number of places available, resulting in waiting lists. A further problem is that there are municipalities that want to pay less and less for this care, says the spokesperson, so that they will no longer be able to deliver the necessary quality. Could the Ministers respond to this message and, if it is correct, how they intend to address the reduction in the number of places available for specialised youth assistance and the waiting lists? Do the ministers recognise that the municipalities want to pay less and less for this concern and how do the ministers view it? Will the temporary mobilisation of 45 million and the proposals of this cabinet be sufficient? Then the secure forensic care. In the report Marktscan Forensische Zorg 2016 of the Dutch Healthcare Authority, I read that policy makers are focusing on more outpatient and lower-security forensic care and on phasing out the higher-security forensic care. The Ministry of Security and Justice agreed to reduce expenditure on forensic care from 688 million in 2013 to 602 million in 2017 with industry organisations GGZ Nederland and the Association of Disabled Care Netherlands. The savings had to be made by reducing the average treatment time of TBs from ten years in 2013 to eight years in 2017, reducing the number of TBs places and promoting the flow to healthcare providers that can provide lower care and stay intensity, such as forensic clinics and departments and providers of protected and supervised living in 2017. Has the average duration for TBs been reduced to eight years in 2017 and has the number of outpatient TBs been increasing? What are the effects of this on the social position and the recurrence of the treated subjects? I am curious to see how ministers intend to monitor whether the forensic care provided is effective for those involved, but also for society, and what ministers see as effective. Is there any change to the new legislation mentioned above? I'd like to hear the Minister's response. Then there is the second point. Is there a connection between the bumping ggz system and the influx into forensic psychiatry? Police have already received 41,082 reports of confused persons in the first half of 2017. Throughout 2016, this number was 74,875. A police chief in the Rotterdam region said in September: "More people die from confused persons than from terror." Care for people with confused behavior has become a matter of justice, police and prison. Former psychiatrist Höppener says in an interview in Trouw of 13 January 2018 that the current care of mental health care is back to what used to be called revolving door psychiatry. Move out as soon as possible. Within the municipal policy, own strength is central. You have to want something of your own and the neighbors have to take care of prevention. The not coming is seen as not wanting. Do the ministers agree with the SP and the Animal Party that there should be primary support and care for confused people and not a cell and, if so, how does the Minister of Health take his role as system responsible? A good conversation with the aldermen who have care in their portfolio seems to the SP Group and the Party for the Animals somewhat thin. I'd like the Minister's response. Is there a connection between the ggz system that fails through waiting lists and runaway psychiatrists and other factors that cause psychiatrically ill people to fall out and then some come back through forensic psychiatry in the ggz? I'd like the Minister's response. The third point concerns the suspect who does not want to participate in a TBS observation. The murders of former Minister Borst and Anne Faber are examples of incidents caused by confused persons. The SP and the Animal Party therefore agree with the government that if suspects suffering from mental illness commit serious crimes, they also need treatment for themselves, but in particular for the protection of society. The legislative proposal makes it possible to recover and use existing medical data for the purpose of investigating the presence of a mental disorder in suspects of crimes for which TBs may be imposed, but who refuse to cooperate with the necessary investigation. The Forensic Care Act thus introduces an exception to the prohibition of the processing of special personal data, as well as a legal obligation to break the medical professional secrecy for emergency workers with whom the suspect has or has had a treatment relationship. The bill suggests that the judge can request the medical file in case a suspect refuses to cooperate in a TBS investigation. In the opinion of the SP and the Party for Animals, the interruption of medical professional secrecy is too heavy and undesirable. As a result, everyone's confidence in doctors is being undermined because medical professional secrecy is being called into question. However, there are alternatives that can have the same or perhaps even a better result. An example of an alternative has been submitted by the Dutch Data Protection Authority. This proposal provides for the law to allow the public prosecutor to ask the court to impose the TBs measure in addition or instead of the prison sentence already imposed within two years of the conviction becoming irrevocable. The SP and the Party for Animals submit to you the proposal of the Personal Data Authority to supplement it in such a way that the convicted person can also ask the judge himself. There may also be considerations of the convicted person to work on himself, for which there is limited scope in detention. Is the government prepared to reconsider this part of the bill and examine the aforementioned alternative? The fourth point is the action of the Health and Youth Inspectorate. In the case of Michael P., the Ministry of Justice and Security and the Health and Youth Inspectorate have been asked by the Minister for Research. This is because of the question from the Lower House and the society how Michael P. could have had so much freedom of movement that it was possible that he robbed Anne Faber of his life. What interests and risk weights have been taken here and has social security been taken into account? The inspections have understood the research question as whether the forensic institution has given good care to the patients. The SP Group finds it incomprehensible that the two inspections did not deal with the questions from the Dutch Parliament and society in the investigation carried out in October and November 2017. I understand there's a second investigation coming up. Why not do it in one go? Several parties in the Lower House, including the SP, the CDA, the VVD, the PVV, GroenLinks and D66, have been complaining about the research reports on the activities of the Health Care Inspectorate for many years. They have repeatedly called on former Minister Schippers to really tackle the Health Inspectorate. I would like to quote the words of the VVD spokesperson in the Lower House: "It is high time for a deep debate on the functioning of the Health Care Inspectorate." What the hell is going on out there, Minister? In the opinion of the SP and the Party for Animals, the government cannot do without a well-functioning Health Care Inspectorate. I'd like to hear the Minister's response. Furthermore, the SP Group would like to know whether the case of Michael P. has resulted in policy changes at the forensic clinics. I'd like to hear the Minister's response. Finally, the final point on after-care. It is important that all those punished after prison and after TBs treatment return to society, for society but also for themselves. An investigation by the Criminal and Youth Protection Board of October 2017 shows that municipalities, prisons and parole authorities are working together poorly in the post-mortem care of prisoners released. Municipalities are often late and poorly informed. Aftercare is intended to prevent prisoners from getting back into trouble after their release. That is a small chance if they get an ID, accommodation, income, care and, if necessary, help in dealing with their debts. But that often goes wrong, for example because municipalities are not told or too late that a prisoner is released, the RSJ concludes. What percentage of inmates leaves prison in a confused state and what is the role of parole in this? I'd like the Minister's response. Furthermore, it was suggested at the expert meeting as an improvement proposal to provide by law that the judge, who decides on the termination of the TBs, can also issue an authorisation under the Law on compulsory mental health care so that there is a seamless transition. What are the ministers' views on this? Could Ministers undertake to examine what can be improved on the care and aftercare of prisoners and T.B.S. to ensure that they are well prepared in society after their release, including the transition from forensic care to regular ggz and mental disability care? The SP Group and the Animal Party look forward to the response of the ministers. Thank you for your time.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u18	Thank you, Mrs Wezel. I give the floor to Mr Schnabel.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u19	For more than a hundred years, the Netherlands has had to do with the Insane Law of 1884 and the introduction of the BOPZ Act in 1994 has also preceded almost a quarter of a century of discussion. And in the almost quarter century since then, the law and implementation practice have been changed several times. Now the intention is to replace the BOPZ Act with three interrelated laws, each of which has a long period of discussion, preparation and modification behind it. These are, of course, also rules on fundamental questions about the limits of fundamental rights. The Law of Insanity was still based on the criterion of good will, which over time, however, was increasingly seen as too broad and too paternalistic at the same time. The BOPZ was, at least initially, based on a very strictly defined hazard criterion, which in practice proved to be too limited, also because the law did provide for compulsory inclusion but not forced treatment, which is contrary to the practice of mental health care of today. As a member of the National Council for Public Health in the 1980s, I was closely involved in advising on the future BOPZ, and I then argued, other than for the goodwill criterion and the danger criterion, for the development of what I then referred to as a common goodwill criterion. This is a way of finding a balance between, on the one hand, concerns about the situation of the patient and, on the other hand, the importance of protecting society and living together. That concept, commonwealth criterion, has not been accepted, but in the practice of the BOPZ and in the legislative proposals currently before us, especially in the Wvggz, there is, in my view, clearly a search for a balance between treatment and security, between caring and limiting danger. The fact that changes in the accents between the two poles can be recognized in the Wvggz's history of development is, in fact, inherent in the nature of the problem to which a solution has to be found. It is always about the extent to which and the moment when the assumption that adults are able to live within the broad rules of what is considered normal or acceptable, at least as not leading to serious harm for the person concerned and for others, at least by those others cannot be sustained. In this situation, of course, there is always the question: who can do what under what conditions and for what purpose? In view of the possible consequences of compulsory care, compulsory inclusion, loss of freedom of movement, the answer to that question cannot and should not be given easily. The Wvggz therefore contains no less than 50 pages of "rules for the provision of compulsory care to a person with mental illness," as the full title of the law reads, followed by another 50 pages of adaptation of other legislation. It is precisely because of this excess that it is not surprising that the VWS Minister had to point out in his letter of 18 December 2017 the "by mistake' in the second letter of amendment to the Wvggz removal of Article 13 (4) which regulates the detection of patients who have withdrawn from compulsory care. If it goes wrong, it is, of course, mainly seen as the failure of the aid and not as an 'abortion' of the legislator. This obviously long unnoticed error is perhaps indicative of the risk posed by precisely the large number of rules laid down in the law, the strict procedures to be followed, the many authorities, disciplines and individuals involved and the decision-making over time and sometimes months. In this way, it can become formally and morally better the enemy of what is practical and clinically seen by professionals in the ggz as the good and necessary. My group therefore welcomes a commitment from the Minister to pay particular attention to this dilemma in the evaluation. This immediately implies that the evaluation should not be based on simply a poll of opinions and experiences after the second year of application of the law, but that from the outset this application should be closely monitored in order to identify what is good and what is not good. The research should actually go along with the application of the law; in the past, you would call that "action research." The insights gained in this way can be used again for the series of AMvBs that is still to be expected. This is not therefore the final evaluation, but the constant response during the first years of application to what is gained in practice. Mr. Chairman. In many respects, the Wvggz represents an improvement on the BOPZ; some of its colleagues have already made this clear. This improvement is also reflected in the much greater attention and appreciation for the patient's social environment. The strengthening of the role of the family in the care process has even given rise to the establishment of a family confidant. A concern, not yet addressed in the law, is either the ratio of this figure to the patient confidence person. I hope that the Minister or the Secretary of State will be able to shed some light on this, because it is not imaginary that these two persons of confidence can be confronted in practice. That does not mean that we can be happy that the time when the patient's family was seen as his most intimate enemy is behind us. The problem is now much sooner that the patients, to a large extent young men, often have no one and nothing to fall back on or get support from. Also the connection with a general practitioner is often totally lacking. This also makes it understandable why the Chief Constable of the National Police, Erik Akerboom, calls for the prevention of the use of the police in a situation that is then out of hand to have a ggz worker in each district. In fact, it is a call for the return of the use of acute and social psychiatry, as it used to be in the big cities. Since the 1930s, the "driving psychiatrist" of the Amsterdam GGD had been following the international and at least much discussed and admired example of this. Today, too: mandatory care in the ggz should be embedded in a system of "intruder care," of permanent contact and contact with those concerned. In the interest of the effectiveness of the Wvggz in particular, my group calls on the VWS Minister to reach the best possible agreements with the municipalities, the police, the ggz and also with the first line, which may ultimately lead to a less frequent appeal to the Wvggz and to the use of the police in some situations. Mr. Chairman. In the long years in which work has been carried out on the BOPZ Act, there has been constant discussion as to whether there should be a common regime for the application of coercion for psychiatric patients, the mentally handicapped and psychogeriatric patients or a separate arrangement for each of the three groups, taking into account the specific characteristics and situation of each of them. It is a bit cynical afterwards to note that the choice of one common regulation proposed in principle in the BOPZ at the time was already abandoned for a very practical reason for the treatment of the law in parliament: the judiciary could be overburdened by the large number of judicial authorisations that would have to be issued in psychogeria under the BOPZ. "He who shuts up agrees" became the new starting point. This was also in line with the practice in the care sector and limited the need for a judicial authorisation to patients who clearly oppose admission to a nursing home. The debate has not been silenced, as it appears today in this house, but it has now become quite remarkablely counter-intuitive. At a time when the separation of compulsion in ggz and compulsion in psychogeriatrics and mentally handicapped people seems to be taking final legal form, there is, on the other hand, a stronger case for a single regulation or at least one framework for the two schemes. This is very clearly the case in ZonMW's Thematic Law Review Forced Care; it has also been mentioned here. The recommendations contained in the report by the Legemaate Committee have now found their way in the recent Leijten amendment, which the VWS Minister now likes to see 'composed' again, as he recently informed this Chamber. I will come back to that in a moment. First of all, I would like to discuss the Health and Force Act without the Leijten amendment. My group sees in the Wzd the further development of the mandatory legal regime applicable to psychogeriatry and mental disability in the context of the BOPZ. That has our support. We can also agree with the assignment to the caretakers for further consultation with their superiors and other experts. As the nature of the care becomes more involuntary and the need for it is also less shared by the person directly involved with the client, the caretaker also needs the consent of other experts, in the extreme case also in plural. However, my group would like to see a clarification by the Minister of his intention in the letter of 18 December to allow the advice of an external expert to take place only if this is necessary for 'good care'. Some colleagues have already pointed out that this could be a problem. Does the Minister refer to the expert referred to in Article 11? Wouldn't making it optional for an external expert to be called in leave too much room for the healthcare professional? Article 25 of that law governs the lodging of an application for a judicial authorisation to reside in an institution. In the case of a psychogeriatric placement, the question arises whether Article 25 (1) (d) also allows the general practitioner to act as an applicant. Who can act as the applicant in the event that a (potential) client does not have any bonds or persons involved? Can neighbors, for example, also act as applicants or the police or the Salvation Army, in the case of someone without permanent residence or residence? We would like to hear the views of the rulers on this. Mr. Chairman. I now come to the Leijten amendment. In his letter of 18 December, the VWS Minister stated that the construct was not in keeping with the practice of disability care, which was almost completely demedicalised a long time ago. The Minister hopes to be able to extend the role of the Wzd doctor to an orthopedagogic or an EZ psychologist. This seems logical and sensible, but the scope of the amendment does not relate to professional expertise as much as to responsibility for "the general course of action in the field of involuntary care." The healthcare provider is obliged to designate a Wzd doctor who is independent and who is not given instructions from the healthcare provider. In fact, this gives the Wzd doctor the position of medical director, as also established at the time by Secretary of State Van Rijn in his response to Mrs Leijten's proposal, which in itself is based on a recommendation from the Legemaate Committee. The character of the law therefore changes somewhat fundamentally. This is, of course, reinforced when this role can also be fulfilled by non-doctors. My group is very keen to hear the Minister's views on this further change in the law, which is also about others and not just about the Wzd doctor, especially in the light of his own proposal. The change proposed by the amendment covers many articles in the legislative proposal and includes, for example, the granting or refusal of leave and dismissal. In fact, the amendment makes a person without a line function in the organisation, possibly even without a job at the organisation, responsible for the establishment of an important part of the care without having to account for it. I hardly have to add that this does not seem to be a very desirable situation. Mr. Chairman. To conclude my discussion of the Health and Force Act, I would like to make a few comments on the concept of Decision care and coercion, which has now been given for consultation. The relevant AMvB, which lays down, in particular, rules on involuntary outpatient care, contains no less than 26 pages, of which 20 pages contain a very detailed explanation of the dos-and-don'ts, even if there is no answer, for example, to the question of whether forced use of, for example, contraception measures is permitted. In a previous existence, this VWS minister clearly had his own view on this. But the real don'ts mainly relate to containment and fixation of psychogeriatric patients. A balcony door may be locked, but not the front door. The wheelchair must not be put on the brake and wristbands to help someone eat are not allowed. I don't mind a bed gate. I don't think it's a stair gate. If you prescribe it in such detail, doesn't the situation threaten that the caregiver shouldn't be allowed to do what is simply done by a caregiver if necessary, possibly even in front of the caregiver? This seems to me to be painful for the helper and certainly not conducive to the caregiver's confidence in the aid. The question is whether you should go so far in detailing these kinds of situations. Involuntary outpatient care is new, as the directors put it themselves in the explanatory memorandum. In the consultation, they shall in particular ask questions as to the desirability of including the use of outpatient containment and fixation. Are there any answers to these questions in that consultation? Can the directors indicate whether and to what extent experimental work has already been carried out in accordance with the proposed directives and, where appropriate, what the results are? Mr. Chairman. The third proposal, the Forensic Care Bill, has been waiting for more than five years for the Supreme Court to decide. Mr. Chairman. The three legislative proposals we are discussing today have become more and more intertwined. That is why we are only now discussing them jointly. ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u20	Thank you, Mr. Schnabel. I now give the floor to Mrs De Bruijn-Wezeman. She's making her maiden speech. That's why I'll ring the bell so everyone knows there's going to be a maiden speech. Mrs. De Bruijn, go ahead.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u21	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I, too, would like to wish the three new rulers every success. It is the first active debate in this Chamber both for them and for me. I don't know about you, but this particular, historic place still makes a very special impression on me. It would silence you if it were not for what brings us together today to call for a wide-ranging debate. After a very long run we finally deal with the three coherent legislative proposals for the Health and Force Act psychogeriatric and intellectually handicapped clients, by me now called the Wzd, the Law mandatory mental health care and the Law forensic care. These three legislative proposals together constitute the legal framework for forced care. The principle is that neither healthcare providers nor public authorities can oblige citizens to undergo certain forms of care. The reality, however, is that there may be very urgent reasons why this concern can provide a solution, even if it is against one's will. As a young nurse in training, for the first time in my life I came into contact with a severely dementia patient. This has made such an impression on me that I still remember this situation now, 45 years later. This lady didn't speak anymore. She couldn't express her will anymore and was also unclear whether she understood anything of what was said to her. She was no longer able to carry out even the simplest actions independently. This meant that she was completely dependent on care and guidance. In doing so, she regularly resisted offering food or drink, against getting out of bed, against toilet help, against combing the hair and so on. Ma'am was entrusted to my care. It was a particular challenge to find access to her world and to learn to read her behavioral expressions as it were. My patient only seemed comfortable when she sat at the table, sitting entirely in her own world, stuck some sort of imaginary stamps. She made the forced gesture all day like she was doing that. I remember that only in that circumstance was she also accessible to eat and drink. I don't remember her name after all these years, but the stamp lady, as I called her affectionately, is just an example of what healthcare professionals have to deal with in everyday practice. Madam President. In our liberal society, it is hard to go against someone's will. Respect for individual rights and human dignity are essential. However, there is a world between respecting resistance and leaving someone to someone's fate. When, in a situation as I have just described, voluntary care no longer provides a solution, it is the legislator's job to provide a legal framework in which the care provider is not embarrassed by law and the legal protection of clients is guaranteed. It is up to this Chamber to assess the legality, enforcement and feasibility of the legislative proposals before us, with particular reference to feasibility, to see whether the three laws are sufficiently compatible and whether the necessary harmonisation has taken place. I shall first give a general view of the three legislative proposals in general and then deal with them separately. I use the following order: Law care and coercion, Law compulsory mental health care and finally the Law forensic care. The members of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats) and European Democrats support the objectives of the legislative proposals. The legislative proposals strike a balance between the fundamental right to physical integrity for each individual and the duty of the government to protect and provide citizens with necessary care. In my group's view, the government is carefully balancing these dilemmas. It is clear that forced care is always an ultimatum remedium, should be carefully prepared, used as short as possible and should be regularly evaluated. When voluntary care no longer provides a solution, forced care as an ultimum remedium should only be applied in situations where a mental disorder leads to such behaviour as to cause a serious disadvantage to the client or his environment. The application of forced and involuntary care is also accompanied by all kinds of judicial safeguards. In the law mandatory ggz is that the issue of a judicial care authorisation in advance and in the law care and coercion is the mandatory inclusion of involuntary care in the care plan, with the possibility of judicial review afterwards. Also in the Wzd involuntary admission requires a prior judicial authorisation. In my group's opinion, these principles and safeguards are compatible with the European Convention on Human Rights and with the requirements of the case law of the European Court of Justice. However, we also know the explanation given by the UN Committee on the Rights of People with Disabilities in Articles 14 and 15 of the UN Convention. That interpretation is such that the UN Committee considers that forced care is in no way justified. Can the directors state again why this interpretation is not followed in the legislative proposals before us? Then I'll come to the Law of care and coercion. The bill was submitted to the House of Representatives in 2009, as has already been recalled today, after which no less than seven notes of amendment were published. In particular, as a result of the second letter of amendment to the compulsory ggz Act, the Wzd has subsequently been substantially amended. A third and a fourth letter of amendment followed. Fortunately, an official note dated 2 June last was very helpful in obtaining any overview. For that, I would like to thank the official organisation. This is without prejudice to the fact that my group understands the request from the field to receive a written explanatory statement. Not only has the overview been lost, but the many changes made to the original legislative proposal have also greatly increased the complexity of implementation practice. And if this is not the right thing to do in this sector, I ask the Minister. At the request of my group, the key concepts in the Wzd and the compulsory ggz Act have been aligned wherever possible. The complaint procedures have been harmonised and the connection in border traffic between the two laws has also been improved. However, the question is whether the harmonisation has not been carried out in such a way as to make the Wzd look like mandatory ggz and whether this is desirable in view of the different target groups. The criticism of the current BOPZ Act is that it does not correspond sufficiently to the care needs of people with intellectual disabilities or with a psycho-geriatric disorder. Contrary to the care targeted at the treatment of the mental disorder in the ggz, the Wzd focuses on care for clients who usually need care throughout their lives and about almost all aspects of their lives. These people often first receive care on a voluntary basis and it is almost inevitable for this target group that there are moments where involuntary care should be provided to prevent the client from seriously harming himself or others. I refer again to the case I have just mentioned. You can then make a slightly better picture of this. So these people are often unable to express their will. It is also not always clear whether someone is resisting. This leads to a different dynamic between client and aid worker. For this reason, the VVD Group supports the choice of procedures that best suit the target group and which lead to an efficient provision of care. In the Wzd this means that involuntary care should be included in the care plan by the caretaker, in consultation with other disciplines. This is in line with the already prescribed practice in the Long-Term Care Act. We therefore believe that a court procedure is not appropriate for this target group, with the exception of the possibility, in the extreme, of taking someone involuntarily. For demented elderly people and their families, the issuing of such a judicial authorisation is also a very emotional event. Despite the very careful approach taken by the judges, the elderly often experience this as a kind of punishment and as if they have done something wrong. However, the question is whether the Wzd still complies with the "effective care" principle after all the changes. The Christmas tree of what has now been called a "scaled multidisciplinary step-by-step plan" has continued to be rigged. The choice of a scaling multidisciplinary roadmap, as the situation becomes more complex and the duration of the involuntary care increases, stems from the recommendations of the 2012 Task Force complex care, but not all care situations are as complex as those on which the recommendations of the Task Force were based at the time. Madam President. Involuntary care is mentioned in the Wzd when the client or his representative does not agree and also when the client can no longer agree or oppose because of willlessness. We now read the law in such a way that in all cases where involuntary care is included in the care plan, external advice should be sought, in addition to the internal disciplinary consultation, involving a doctor in many forms of care. You will understand, I think, that my group will embrace the amendment announced by the Minister in his letter of 19 December last to increase the practical workability of the Roadmap when it actually leads to less bureaucratic decision-making processes. We agree with the proposed line of restricting the consultation of an external expert, for example to situations where the evaluation of the care plan shows that it is not possible to reduce involuntary care within the legal deadline. We ask the Minister to be closely involved in the preparation of the amendment ActiZ and the Association of Disabled People in the Netherlands and to check with them whether the proposed approach is feasible and fits within the policy aimed at reducing rules and regulatory pressure. Then I come to another point. In the explanatory memorandum, the fact that someone has an increased risk of falling is expressly excluded from the serious disadvantage criterion. Measures to prevent falls, such as a seat blade, can therefore never be used involuntarily, not even with the consent of the representative. During my contacts with the field, it appears that this is a hard-to-understand story both for the people working in the care sector and for family members. This is of course not strange, as it is known that in 2015, for example, 3,267 elderly people over 65 years of age died of the consequences of a fall. Then, according to the Minister, as a health care provider, how do you consider the criterion to be a serious disadvantage? I'm making a comparison. The number of suicides was 1.871 in the same year. So that's just over half of it. Should it not also be possible to apply the "no' principle in the event of an increased risk of fall? Now, by definition, it is 'no' if we want to take preventive measures. In addition, the death after a fall is considered a non-natural death. If a person dies in a care relationship after a fall, it doesn't matter whether that happens in an institution or at home, a police investigation will follow in which the persons concerned are heard. Moreover, the chances of this happening and happening are increasing because prevention measures can no longer be taken without the consent of the client, and thus incompetent patients. I don't think it's realistic to mentor clients one-on-one 24 hours a day. Such a police investigation has a huge impact on both the family and the caregivers. A care manager of an institution that is working very actively on the cultural change to deal consciously with freedom-limiting measures that is cultural change has already been recalled a few times today..... recently told me that after such a police investigation the chairs that had just been banned were again widely deployed within that institution. The former Secretary of State mentioned a necessary cultural change in which aid workers had to resist their reflexes in order to protect others against their will against risks. But is such a change of culture not necessary in the whole width? If we accept the risk that, in care relationships, older people who are no longer able to agree to measures will fall and die from them, should we not find a cultural change in how we, as a society, deal with them? A police investigation in connection with a non-natural death doesn't help. This does not mean respecting the client's right to self-determination and physical integrity, but rather mistrust towards caregivers. At least this is how carers and family members experience it. I would like to hear the Minister's position on this. Finally, I go into the block about the Wzd on the Wzd doctor. During the treatment in the Lower House, this doctor was added to the Wzd by Mrs Leijten's amendment. Mr. Don mentioned that, too. For the time being, disability care seems to be the main obstacle, as many freedom-limiting measures are used on the basis of behavioural considerations and not on medical grounds. If a doctor has to take the final responsibility, this is not in line with current practice. Here too, the Minister proposes to adapt the Wzd, in the sense that the function of the Wzd doctor can also be exercised by an EZ psychologist or an academicly trained orthopedagogue. We think this is a good proposal in itself, but we also ask the Minister to pay particular attention to the added value of the Wzd doctor in the review of the law. To what extent does this additional link in the decision-making process contribute to the quality and efficiency of care? Spokesmen for me have already indicated how the complexity of the Wzd doctor relates to several articles in the Wzd. I would like to take this into account in the evaluation. I now come to the mandatory ggz Act. In addition to strengthening the legal status of persons against whom care is provided, the legislative proposal for the mandatory ggz aims to prevent coercion and limit the duration of coercion. Reduction of compulsion is also made possible by the use of lighter forms of compulsory care. The aim is, in turn, to be able to intervene at an earlier stage in order to prevent a client's mental health from getting worse in such a way that compulsory inclusion or a crisis measure becomes necessary. These objectives can count on the support of the VVD Group. However, the VVD Group regrets that the safeguards for the application of mandatory care in home situations are not directly included in the law and that we have to wait for the AMvBs, which, by the way, are subject to a pending procedure. As a result, we now have insufficient insight into the feasibility of the outpatient treatment options. This is one of the key elements of this law. By letter dated 26 March 2008, the Minister promised additional due diligence when applying mandatory care in home situations. For example, it is indicated that a health care professional should monitor measures restricting freedom. We would like to hear from the Secretary of State what possibilities he sees for compulsory care in the home situation. Is the Secretary of State not concerned that due to the lack of sufficient professional supervision it is necessary to decide on forced care? Reduction of compulsion is, as has been said, an important objective of the Law mandatory ggz. We also want to prevent a person from entering the area of criminal law or public order unnecessarily as a result of mental illness. The quality and organisation of mental health care are not in itself part of the compulsory ggz Act, but cannot be completely separated from the scope of coercive measures. It is therefore appropriate to consider in this context the need for adequate provision of care, preferably in an outpatient environment, in its own familiar environment. Over the years, clinical ggz capacity has been steadily reduced, but the build-up of adequate outpatient care remains insufficient. It is good that there is now a Switch team for people with confused behavior that is committed to achieving sufficient local, comprehensive care offerings. An important part of this is the early alert and guidance to appropriate care and support. The municipalities have an important role to play here. We are getting signals that the municipalities do not feel equipped enough for this. For example, involving a client's environment in mandatory care often encounters privacy legislation. The municipality should also take into account, examine and reconnect reports concerning persons who may be required to receive mandatory mental health care. Can the Secretary of State undertake to implement this section carefully with the municipalities? Then I come to the active role of the Public Prosecutor, which we have asked questions about before. In the meantime, the First Chamber received a copy of the letter of 6 July of the then Secretary of State for Security and Justice, which was addressed to the Second Chamber. Then there is a topic on which we have recently been reminded and which has a clear relationship with the legislative proposals that are before us today: the legal representation of unqualified adults in health care. More or less grown up with the WGBO, the Medical Treatment Agreement Act, I don't know better than that in care relationships a doctor determines whether someone is willing or not. This must then be based on a capacity of will until proven otherwise. Willingness is a task-specific concept in health legislation, linked to a particular type of decision at a given time. So it is already included in the Wzd and in the compulsory ggz Act. This does justice to one's ability to make decisions at certain moments and fronts. The doctor shall consider whether a person is able to come to a reasonable appreciation of his interests in this matter. If a patient is incompetent for a decision in this matter, the patient may appoint a representative himself, or the canton judge appoints a curator or mentor. The mentoring is a protection measure that has been explicitly introduced for health care activities and is regulated by the Civil Code. For example, it provides that the person concerned may no longer take decisions on treatment, guidance, care and nursing through the institution of the mentoring institution itself. He may only do so with the agreement of the curator or mentor. So even if the doctor considers that the person concerned can act in this situation, he should not act without the permission of the curator or the mentor. However, the mentor may authorise the person concerned to carry out legal acts himself. However, it is up to the mentor and not to the doctor to determine whether the person concerned is capable of a reasonable appreciation of his interests. I ask the directors: is there any inconsistency between the above-mentioned laws? Are the directors familiar with problems between caregiver and curator or mentor who find their basis in this apparent contradiction? Finally, Mr President, the amendment to Article 37a of the Code of Criminal Law concerning the non-observatories included in the draft law on forensic care. The aim of this scheme is to solve the problem that suspects of serious crimes refuse to cooperate in the investigation for the purpose of the pro Justitia report on the presence of a mental disorder. If suspects do not cooperate, it becomes very difficult for the NIFP to come up with an opinion. The defendant refusing to cooperate tries to avoid the imposition of a possible TBs measure. This is a very undesirable and dangerous situation, because it means that serious offenders do not receive the treatment they need, with all the risk of recurrence. Article 37a contains a legal breach of medical professional secrecy, and medical professional secrecy is of major importance. There is a need for extreme restraint in proposing their legal breakdown. However, medical confidentiality is not absolute. For example, as part of the investigation, there are already possibilities to obtain the seizure of medical records in very exceptional circumstances. It is now up to us to assess whether the intended breach of medical professional secrecy as an ultimate remedy for the situation in which the imposition of TBs is deemed necessary for security reasons is proportionate and sufficiently guaranteed. The non-observatories scheme applies only to suspects associated with very serious crimes for which an unmaximised TBs can be imposed. This concerns acts of violence within the meaning of Article 38e of the Code of Criminal Law. In particular, life crimes and serious sexual offences are covered. With regard to such offences, the European Court of Human Rights has also expressly considered that the State has a so-called positive obligation to take preventive measures to prevent this type of crime wherever possible. It is very important that suspects of this type of crime find out whether there is a mental disorder. Where this is the case, there may be a high risk of recurrence and the imposition of a TBS measure may be the most appropriate sanction. In this sense, we consider the measures to be proportionate in order to achieve this. With regard to the due diligence criteria regarding the breach of medical professional secrecy, the former Secretary of State stated that the obligation of confidentiality was established on the entire multidisciplinary committee that checks the usability of the personal data. Can the Minister provide additional information on how enforcement is organised? Does ordinary disciplinary law apply to this, for example? We also have another question. At present, the main rule is that behavioral reports at the time the hearing starts should not be older than one year. How does this principle relate to the period of validity of the data to be collected for the PJ reporting? Madam President...
