ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u27	I was just getting some water, Mr. Chairman. Excuse me. Mr. Chairman. It is an honor and a pleasure to be able to make this contribution on behalf of the OSF. I, too, would like to welcome the leaders of VWS and Justice and Security to the Upper House. This is their first or second performance here, and they're going straight into the deep end. But I think the swimming degrees are fine. I look forward to a debate, hopefully, through and through. From this point on, our groups would also like to thank all those concerned in the previous cabinet for their efforts for these three laws. We did not and do not always agree with their proposals on this later, but they worked with passion and commitment to these laws without exception, until the first statement of response. Others have already mentioned it: in 1994, after more than twenty years of discussion, the Act entered into force on special admissions into psychiatric hospitals. This law replaced the 19th-century insanity law and law of weakness, and had to be a codification of the radically changed insights since the 1960s and 1970s on good care and rights of people with severe psychiatric disorders or intellectual disabilities. Not that the discussion after the adoption of the BOPZ Act became silent, quite the contrary. The second evaluation of the BOPZ Act of 2002 concluded that the law did not comply in practice, so fundamental that it could not be repaired with adaptation. I will come back to this later. The fact is that the laws that are before us today for final assessment here in the First Chamber have been discussed for more than fifteen years. Our groups have neither the illusion nor the wish that this discussion will end after the votes on the Law mandatory ggz, the Law care and coercion and the Law forensic care. We regard these laws as human rights laws, which deal with very important issues. When may you affect people who are caused by mental illness or mental impairments that can seriously harm themselves or others, for their own sake or out of the interest of others, in their self-determination or the integrity of their bodies, and force them to receive care or treatment? Under what conditions should fundamental rights and civil liberties of people with mental impairment or mental impairment be set aside, while they have done nothing wrong? And if they have done something wrong, caused by their illness or disability, how do we provide them with appropriate care, and society with the necessary security, and prevent it from happening again? There's an inherent tension in these questions, and that's good. The provision of forced care is by definition accompanied by dilemmas. For example, we do not see any black-and-white discrimination between human rights and forced care. Thousands of people per year show that the human dignity of their existence is served precisely by the application of forced care. Back to the years in which people from far too absolute views of self-determination were slackened and destitute, we do not want, as much as we desire back to the time when psychologically ill or intellectually limited people were standardly locked up or isolated from society. Seriously ill or limited people are also fellow human beings, with basically the same needs, rights and freedoms as everyone else. There is no final answer to the question of how their care needs are best balanced with civil rights. The views on this subject are subject to social change and new insights on the question of what good care is. And every situation in which this legislation is applied is unique, because it is always about an individual, a person with a unique mix of circumstances, limitations, predisposition and possibilities. In practice, there is also tension between the rights and freedoms of patients and clients and the safety of healthcare professionals. In some institutions, a daily balance has to be made between good employership and the requirements of the Arbowet, and the rights and needs of patients and clients. That's a heavy load. The best way to deal with this kind of complex dilemma is, according to our groups, to talk about it permanently. Standing debate keeps the questions sharp, and the answers that need to be given in practice on a case-by-case basis. Without such a conversation, we are by definition failing the people who are the direct object of these laws. The provision of forced care must never be a solidified procedure or the completion of a bureaucratic check-off list. The questions underneath are too fundamental for that. Today, however, the question lies with us whether the law on compulsory ggz, the law on care and compulsion and the law on forensic care will now form a solid and workable legal framework for the complex trade-offs that the care is faced with on a daily basis. Let me say first of all that we regard these laws largely as a visible step forward in relation to the BOPZ Act. The rights of patients and their families are better defined. Ambulant forced care is possible, so that forced withdrawal, which is often a traumatic experience, can be prevented more often. The laws encourage informed consideration of the need for forced care and its reduction as soon as possible. The coherence of these laws is also important for our group. The Senate has had a special responsibility for this since the adoption in 2004 of the Van de Beeten and Others motion, which called on the government to strengthen the coherence between forensic and regular care in order to prevent crime and recidivism. The laws before us today can be regarded as a direct consequence of that motion on parts. It has already been mentioned that the Wfz has already reached its first term here in 2014. But the treatment was stopped at the time to wait for the Wzd and the Wvggz, so that the three laws could be judged by us as a senate. We cannot come to a conclusion other than that this decision of our Chamber has had a beneficial effect on the connection between the three laws. Since then, around 2016, the government has undertaken to define many concepts by letter of amendment the same, and that has visibly improved the laws. Key concepts such as "care', "care provider', "care provider' and "accommodation', complaint procedures and compensation schemes, internal quality assurance, the further development of outpatient care in AMvBs: it is all harmonised. Moreover, both the Wzd and the Wvggz now have the same criterion of "serious disadvantage" to assess whether there can be any question of forced care at all. That is, of course, crucial for a good connection. It does raise a further question. The government has repeatedly indicated that replacing the concept of "danger" in the BOPZ Act as grounds for forced care by "serious disadvantage" in the new laws does not mean any material change and is in line with case law. On the contrary, we are inclined to think that the step from 'danger' to'serious disadvantage' is a good substantive step, which is better suited to what people need to care in practice. Can the Secretary of State explain this and explain how this has developed in case law in such a way that it now amounts to the same material? Also the way the motion-Van de Beeten c.s. concretely completed with the connection between regular and forensic care, can count on our support. There has also been the necessary discussion in this House, in particular in writing, on Article 2.3 of the Wfz, which provides that the criminal court may impose an authorisation for forced care in accordance with the criteria of the Wvggz and the Wzd. We welcome the Government's response in the response. We also consider this provision to be an enrichment of the instruments available to the criminal court. It offers the possibility of delivering customised work, even during or after detention. Provided that it is properly and carefully applied, this provision can prevent misery for society and for the person concerned themselves. It exudes what these laws should radiate in our opinion, namely that society does not let go of those who need care: we stay with you and provide tailored care, in order to prevent yourself, whether or not again, from slipping and causing serious harm to yourself or to other people. That this provision is no longer in the Code of Criminal Law, but in the Wfz we find, in principle, with the government the only right choice, and is precisely what the Van de Beeten motion asked for at the time. It was the previous rulers who wrote that in the reply. We would like to give the new rulers the room to confirm this. Do they, too, strongly support this provision? And, in their view, is all the conditions for the provision to succeed in practice? Will judges be sufficiently equipped to actually apply Article 2.3 Wfz? Have all the problems in the connection between forensic care and the Health Insurance Act been resolved? I have the impression not, but I'd like to give the government a chance to explain. Are there sufficient treatment places available to provide people with a suitable place under Article 2.3 Wfz? AEF's report didn't say so this spring. Has the situation improved? Legally, by letter of amendment, the Wzd is better connected to this article in the Wfz, but is disability care also sufficient as a sector in view? We miss the VGN at various consultation tables. It is estimated that more than half of the prisoners have intellectual disabilities, so there is much profit to be made there. Does the government share that? And is the parole also sufficiently connected? Mr. Chairman. So far, therefore, the assessment of the coherence between the three laws by our political groups is positive. But we stayed with one big question, namely whether it was a right choice to split the BOPZ Act into the Wvggz and the Wzd. This means, in particular, external legal protection when and how can a person be brought into compulsory care? It is also important to ensure that there is no discrimination between people in ggz on the one hand and care for the elderly on the other. We wonder whether this is desirable and sustainable. This issue has already been discussed in the written exchange of views and we are not yet convinced by the Government's response. In short, I would like to be corrected if I go too short, in other words, that answer is that, according to the government, the external legal protection in Wzd and Wvggz does indeed differ formally, but that material amounts to the same. For example, in both laws, forced care can only be applied as an ultimatum remedium, if it is proportional and subsidiary and if it is phased out as soon as possible. Although forced care in the Wvggz must always and in every form be subject to prior judicial review and the Wzd knows, except in the case of forced admission, a step-by-step plan with only ex post judicial review, but these different forms of legal protection are not inferior to each other, the government still argues. They are also justified, because they result from differences in target groups. Mandatory care in the ggz is aimed at recovery and healing and usually short-lived, where psychogeriatric forced care and forced care for people with intellectual disabilities is usually offered life-wide and, from a given point of view, life-long. A court ruling prior to any application and/or adaptation of forced care in disability and elderly care would therefore be too burdensome, both for clients and their relatives and for the judiciary. Moreover, in the latter two sectors, according to the government, people are generally incompetent, where people in the ggz tend to be able to express their opposition to care. Finally, the Government refers to the second evaluation of the BOPZ Act, which would already indicate that one law with a single scheme for everyone does not fit in with the care of people with dementia and severe intellectual impairment. We recognise the difference in character mentioned by the government between the care, whether forced or not, in ggz, care for the elderly and care for the elderly. But in our view the most fundamental improvement of Wvggz and Wzd compared to the BOPZ Act is that forced care will no longer have to be limited to forced admission, so that customization can be offered per patient and client and stepped care is possible. This makes that distinction in the nature of care between those sectors smaller rather than larger. We do not want to clean up those differences. They are there and they are real................................................................................................................................................................................................... What does the government think of that thought? The division of the BOPZ Act also makes it possible for a doctor, not the legislator, to determine which regime of external legal protection a person will be subject to. This is a situation which, to put it mildly, we do not consider optimal when it comes to human rights. The least willing people are the most difficult to express resistance. And without resistance, there is no forced care at all. They are therefore the most vulnerable to a situation in which carers argue against resistance in order to maintain that the actually forced care they apply is voluntary. We consider it undesirable for those same caregivers to decide whether or not their choices for a client should be reviewed in advance by an independent judge. It carries the risk of arbitrariness and of "thinking for people." In any case, we do not want to detract from the good intentions with which this is undoubtedly happening, but it may mean that unfair or excessive care is being applied. We ask the government whether the inspection of forced care under the Wzd will pay particular attention to this risk. The government's supposed distinction between Wzd and Wvggz in willpower also, in our opinion, does not do enough justice to the fluent flow of the capacity to express resistance to people in vg-care and psychogeriatric care. In one situation, someone with learning difficulties can make it clear what he does not want and not in the other. In a person with dementia, that can even be different at a moment's notice. Building two distinct laws with different external legal protection regimes on a basis that is mobile and situational in individual people is like building a house on quicksand. And it is once again the health care provider, not the legislator, who in practice has to turn it into solid ground. Finally, two laws with different regimes can cause the most vulnerable people to fall between shore and ship or not to receive appropriate care. We are very concerned about that. People with intellectual disabilities and psychiatric illness need complex, integral care. They have not been helped by two laws which emphasise the distinction between sectors. What is the risk that, for example, severely addicted people with intellectual disabilities and/or any other mental disorder will be sent from the locker to the wall? It's been going on for years in our country, and unfortunately it's still happening. Would the government like to address all these concerns about breaking up Wzd and Wvggz? I hope I have been able to clarify how deep these are, but we are fully prepared to be persuaded. Mr. Chairman. That requires the government to come up with stronger arguments than in the response. For example, she stated that the choice of two laws for different sectors is the result of the second evaluation of the BOPZ Act. Looking for reasons to remove our concerns, we took them up ourselves, because I was curious about the argumentation. And what does it turn out to be? The second evaluation does not call for different laws for different sectors, but for specific legislation, whether or not within a single law, for people who do not show a willingness to take care but who do not object, the so-called gbbb group; an ugly care discount. According to the second evaluation, these people are overlooked in the BOPZ Act, but the evaluation also emphasizes that the gbgb group can also be found in the long term ggz, where not everyone is willing enough to express resistance explicitly. Somewhere this nuance of the second evaluation has been lost since 2002. We regret that this has happened. We would have had better legislation today if it had been held. What will not convince us in this debate is a lack of capacity in the Public Prosecutor's Office and in the judiciary to also be able to assess and test out out outpatient forced care in the context of the Wzd. We are certainly sensitive to the argument that not every step-by-step plan or every scaling-up of forced care according to the Wzd can or should pass the judge. But the fact that the judge never gets involved until there is a forced admission is the other extreme. Surely, at least once, there could and should be a principled, independent legal judgement as to whether there should be an issue of outpatient forced care or forced treatment in a person? If there is not enough capacity in the State and the judiciary for this, then it must be made. These are political choices, I admit, but these are human rights safeguards, which, in the view of the Labour and OSF groups, deserve priority. To conclude this point, the Government also states in the reply that it does not wish to distinguish between heavy and light forced care in the context of WZD, since the degree of experience of compulsoryity is subjective and individual. That sounds nice and it's definitely true. But for the legislator it is inconsistent and just too easy. For the government apparently finds forced admission, even in the Wzd, to go so far as to require prior judicial review. Why then, for example, is not forced medication, fixation or isolation also brought under prior review by the judge? These are interventions that, like forced admission, affect a person's ability to resist, and that seems to us to be a very workable criterion for determining whether there is severe forced care or not. How does the Minister feel about that? And does he agree with us that the legislator should certainly have a view on what heavy and light forced care is? Let us be clear: our group has not yet decided whether the distinction in external legal status between the compulsory ggz and Wzd Act is desirable and workable and we doubt whether the choices in the Wzd are the right ones. At the same time, as has been said, we believe that the Wzd is a visible and significant step forward in relation to the current law. But he's not what he could have been. In an ideal world, we believe that there is a single legal framework for forced care, which includes forced care in the somatics and the coherent discussions on willlessness, as well as the criteria laid down in the Law of Principles. Does the government share that vision of the future? Is she prepared to carry out a study on this? Perhaps the rulers will now think: then we will all be back here in fifteen years' time. However, we believe that this fits perfectly with the ongoing debate we want on care and coercion. As far as the present Wzd is concerned, we are not yet clear on how our final judgment will fall. But again, we like to be convinced. So I would very much like to have a thoughtful reaction from the rulers who provide us, the groups of the Labour Party and OSF, with new insights. Which brings me to the Forensic Care Act. The procurement of forensic care by the JenV Ministry and all its predecessors in the names "forensic care" that is regulated herein, has been going on for almost ten years now. In practice, the government points out, in particular, the organisation of continuity of care is not yet as desired. A policy programme with social partners should improve. This was one of the places where we missed involvement of the VGN. Can we see that right? If so, why aren't they at the table? In 2014, our colleague Jannette Beuving already indicated that we can support the Wfz for the most part. But a number of issues have remained; they are hard on our stomachs. They have already been mentioned by colleagues today. First, it concerns the powers conferred on the Minister of JenV by the Wfz to intervene unilaterally in private law institutions carrying out a public service mission in the implementation of TBs. That minister can even intervene directly, ignoring the board of directors. That's a long way to go. These are private-law institutions which are not hierarchically subordinate to the Minister; in our view, the government must respect an appropriate distance. If the government plays the purchasing role, it is not appropriate for the same government to intervene unilaterally if it does not like the outcome of negotiations. To give just one concrete example: it may happen that the Minister, because he has to cut costs, sets the rates for forensic care too low. Institutions cannot circumvent this, because they are dealing with only one buyer. If these too low rates reduce the quality of care, the same minister who bought under the cost price can move the board of directors aside. That is not a fair situation with equal negotiating partners. As far as we are concerned, the Minister should have chosen: either he will continue to regulate the implementation of forensic care administratively, with all the consequences for the connection to regular care, but with the appropriate powers to intervene; or he will organise the quality and development of care he desires by buying in properly and purposefully. Why has the government not used all these letters of amendment in recent years to make a choice? We are all the more concerned about this because the government has indicated to our written questions that it has not encountered a single situation in which it urgently needed these powers in the last ten years. The Minister always came out in consultation with institutions. And that is the way it should be, as we are asking the Minister today. The second point that we have unabated difficulty with at the Wfz concerns the possibility of setting aside the medical confidentiality of non-observatories. A radical measure, of which we wonder whether it is unnecessary and counterproductive. Isn't it true that judges can impose on them without Pro Justitia reporting? Isn't there a bill coming that doubles the observation time at the Pieter Baan Center? And hasn't the average length of stay in the TBs fallen visibly? This is an important fact, because the ever-increasing length of residence in the TBs up to a few years ago threatened to affect the legitimacy of the whole system. In the reply, the government still speaks of "other measures" to promote the cooperation of suspects. Which one does the government mean? We have received it as solidly as creative advice from Mr Wolfsen from the Personal Data Authority. Does the Minister not think that this is a good proposal and a workable alternative to the plan that is now under way? Will all this not provide sufficient relief, so that the draconian measure of breaking up medical professional secrecy is not necessary? In our view, it balances on the brink of the rule of law that no one has to contribute to their own condemnation. Unlike the government, we see risks of counterproductive effects: the risk that people will avoid care before they commit a crime because of the risk that the requested treatment will ever turn against them. The government doesn't see that happening, because I'm putting it back together again, and people aren't that calculated. Unfortunately, we can think of examples where that would be the case. I would like to mention, for example, pedosexuals, who often have an understanding of illness, or people with a sociopathic or narcissistic personality disorder who are encouraged by their environment to seek help. Isn't it always better, for example, to ask the government to make maximum efforts to prevent crimes rather than to obtain a desired conviction more easily after the fact? Then why isn't this so controversial part of the Forensic Care Act not abandoned?

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u46	Mr. Chairman. I was in the process of explaining the motion in part No. EK 28.979, letter E, by Rob van de Beeten, who is present here. It was then considered that the detention of addicts with mental problems, psychiatric patients and mentally disabled is increasingly a problem in the prison system and that a penitentiary regime for the majority of these prisoners is unsuitable for the necessary treatment and rehabilitation. It was also considered that for this group of prisoners targeted cooperation and coordination between care institutions, assistance, justice, employment services which were still there, I have just said that there are serious gaps between municipalities and municipalities. The Inter-departmental Working Group-Houtman and the very interesting and very readingable parliamentary research (the 2005-2006 meeting) tried to respond to these shortcomings in the systems at the time. Madam President, the parliamentary inquiry I have just referred to led to an incident in 2005. An incident in which an escaped TB's committed a serious crime. It was the second time that year. The current TBS system, as was said at the time, is insufficiently capable of protecting society in its present form. At the same time, that was the task of investigating the committee. It was also particularly interesting to note that this report has dealt extensively with politics and media logic. Even now, we are dealing with this, while learning mistakes, it is open to improvements more than ever. The legislation before us today seeks to balance interests better and to strike a balance between the protection of society on the one hand and the interests of the patient on the other. I shall go back to the distant past, as has just been done for a while. A hundred years ago, the predecessors of the three legislative proposals were only on certain provisions in the Civil Code and on a provisional and brief arrangement according to the Royal Decree of 11 April 1818. In 1841, a special Law on Insanity came into force, which, after a number of interim amendments, resulted in special admissions to psychiatric hospitals (Wet BOPZ) in 1992. This law, in turn, was eventually replaced by the Law on compulsory mental health care (Wvggz). When we look at the legal history, it is very clear that the patient's interest is becoming increasingly central. In 1818 the starting point was that society had to be protected from the sufferer. There was hardly any regard for the needs and for the well and woe of the patient. The law of 1841 significantly improved the daily care of patients, and it was called "sustainable" and that was a new word about places to promote recovery. The recording should then be based on a court ruling. The patient himself, family, the prosecutor and treating physician was mentioned that when the nerve doctor was given a major role in the request for admission. The change in society's perception of the importance of the patient and his treatment gradually became apparent. But even then, this legislation came about after a long and very difficult incubation process, in which solutions were always sought for several weaknesses of existing laws. It was 1884 when provinces were given responsibility for the presence of sufficient nursing homes, when state supervision was introduced into the law and mayors were given the power to put someone immediately in custody when there is a serious suspicion that someone presents such an immediate threat, not only to themselves but also to public order. So that was the new law. The proposals now before us have also been very intensive and lengthy, but in the opinion of the CDA Group, this has meant that there really is a better proposal. Care is central and no longer the forced admission. Forced care as an ultimatum remedium: only if all alternatives are exhausted in a voluntary framework and forced care is the last means to avert severe disadvantage, then coercion is allowed, and this forced care can now also be provided out of work. That is the starting point and its relevance is endorsed by the CDA Group. Then I come to the importance of the client, the patient, the person concerned, as previously noted. Moreover, would it not be appropriate for me to ask that question for the legibility of the legislation when the three legislative proposals also use a similar terminology? The patient's interest is great. The changes in the law will increase the emphasis on his well-being. At the same time, the importance of security for society as such is at least as great. Unfortunately, as a result of the disorder, people not only cause themselves serious harm, but also pose a threat to others or to social security. I'll start with the Wet forensic care. It's a framework law. The contents of the forensic care are not described here. Patients should be directed to the right care. After all, it is about the right placement and also about ensuring possible transfer. The objectives of these legislative changes are clear: sufficient available, timely, appropriate and high-quality forensic care................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................... It is precisely this that reduces repeated accidents and improves safety. In short: patients in the right place with the right care. In addition, the connection between the penal system and the curative ggz is improved. The adaptation of the control system and the financing of the TBS will prevent persons with mental impairment or mental impairment from entering a judicial establishment where they do not belong. The fact that things can go horribly wrong has recently turned out to be true again. Tackling events continue to fuel public pressure and perhaps also the political will to keep the safety of society in mind and less the individual interest of the patient. In this light, the CDA Group understands the scheme of non-observants. It is justifiable that a suspect of a tbs-worthy crime who, either on the recommendation of the defense, or as a result of the suspect's pathology, refuses to cooperate in the pro-justitia investigation can still be imposed on tbs. The amendment proposed by this law to Article 37a of the Criminal Code makes it possible to use existing medical data of reluctant observers to answer the question of whether a mental disorder existed at the time of committing the crime. This regulation may provide for an urgent social need, taking into account the requirements of proportionality, subsidiarity and efficiency, as well as the compliance of the regime with our fundamental rights, in the interests of medical professional secrecy. On Friday we received a letter from Mr Wolfsen, chairman of the Dutch Data Protection Authority, who has devised another variant for the refusing observers. I would like to hear a response from the government to that. At first sight, I can see that a lot of legislation needs to be changed, but perhaps that is the reason to say: we have a first judgment and we will take it with us when we have an evaluation. Madam President. We also saw that a safe and responsible transfer between the different domains of forensic care, prison system, forensic ggz and tbs, requires the utmost care, taking into account the risk of social unrest, escape and recidivism, the level of security needed and the presence of suitable places at all institutions. The Minister has now indicated that the implementation of the Wfz chain approach will be less extensive than that of the other laws. Mr. Chairman. I come to the Law mandatory ggz. With regard to this law, I can be brief. The new law is much more focused on preventing mandatory care and opting for less coercion. There is an eye for the unintended, long-term harmful effects of mandatory care. This indeed fits in with the present image of self-determination, human integrity and dignity. Then the Health and Force Act psychogeriatric and mentally handicapped clients. This legislative proposal, aimed at people with dementia, the elderly and those with learning disabilities, is based on the principle that coercive measures do not apply; 'no, unless' rather than 'who does not speak up'. Here too, forced care should only be applied when all alternatives to voluntary care have been tested and involuntary care is the only way to prevent serious harm, and then only under responsible supervision, not only within institutions but also now that care can be provided elsewhere, with the elderly, the disabled or the sick home. ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u47	Thank you, Mrs Oomen. I give the floor to Mr Ganzevoort.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u48	Mr. Chairman. For our part, too, congratulations to Mr De Bruijn-Wezeman and a warm welcome to the ministers and the Secretary of State, who are making their debut today with such important legislative proposals, at least in this cabinet period in this Chamber. My group would like us to be able, once again, to raise the debate on care to a higher level and to discuss what really matters, namely ensuring optimal and accessible care for those who need it. It is always very tempting to fall into technocratic and bureaucratic discussions or into easy-going populist talk that does not do justice to the complicated reality. After all, it is about how we deal with the complex dilemmas of care, care that we want to give to people who need it and who sometimes do and sometimes do not ask for it. It's about people like my mom, who's slowly demented and can decide a lot of things for themselves, but not everything. It's about the people with sometimes severe mental and physical disabilities that my sister as a doctor is responsible for and who often can't decide for themselves what's good for them. It's about people with psychiatric disorders or addictions who sometimes know very well what they want but can't realize it, whether they want something that's problematic for themselves or for others. It is about people who commit serious crimes or who could possibly commit them again under the influence of their limitation or disorder, when we must ensure safety for themselves, but above all for innocent people in their environment, without taking unnecessarily rigid draconian measures. In other words: it is always about dilemmas in the care, and even more so when we talk about forced care. That is, in a sense, an internal conflicting term, and perhaps not. Mr Oomen has just said this in a different way. Care is basically to enter into a care relationship in which you are fundamentally equal, you take responsibility for caring for the other and you do what they need. At the moment when compulsion comes into play, it is at least absolved by the equivalent care relationship. If the other can resist or can't even indicate that he or she wants to, it's all the more pressing. But there can be no escape from that either, because no intervention can lead to much greater suffering. That is the dilemma that these legislative proposals address in an important way. The core of our assessment will therefore be whether the coercion is indeed limited to the minimum and whether the rights of the forced person are protected as far as possible. In addition, we will have to ask whether the way in which the dilemmas are weighed up is not organised so heavily that it will lead to a reduction in the quality of care. My group is pleased that we are dealing jointly with the three laws. This has led to some delays, but it also means that we can take a good look at the coherence. The reality of forced care is complex. That starts with the question of which law applies where and when. What do we do with a client with intellectual disabilities, who also develops psychiatric problems? What do we do with the fact that inmates often have a slight mental disability and/or psychiatric problems above average? What do we do if someone with psychotic disorders commits a crime? It is precisely this overlap that makes it difficult to develop a clear legal framework. That is why my group's first question to the government is to clarify this. On the one hand, it has often been said in the discussion that the present problem must determine the legal framework to be used. They're personal and non-locational laws. That makes sense, but the question is whether that is workable. In practice, we see that in the case of inpatient care, the institutional setting is not the problem at hand. Institutions for care of the elderly or for the mentally handicapped count on the Law on care and compulsion, even when clients develop a mental disorder there that may require forced care. Ggz institutions will use the Compulsory ggz Act, even if there is a clear psychogeriatric disorder or intellectual disability. In the forensic setting one will always see the forensic aspect as the present. Our question is: doesn't the division into three laws actually mean that the institutional framework in which care is provided determines the scope of the law being applied? This is problematic, because in both young people and adults, it sometimes seems to depend on chance in which sector or sub-area someone goes for care. In the adult patient, the first indication of the disease is extremely important. Sometimes that is determined by the discipline of the intaker alone. In the case of youth ggz, strict criteria are applied, which means that many young people with mental illness who could best be treated in the youth ggz, instead end up in youth care institutions or judicial youth institutions. In the forensic ggz, imposition of a measure and placement are increasingly being separated and people in the forensic psychiatric centers are being treated by the reduced number of people made available on the basis of other legal bases, and thus with different legal bases and forms of funding. Relocation can often not take place quickly enough, so that the current problem is not always consistent with the institution where someone is staying. If all this is so in practice now, should we not just be clear and honest and not talk too highly about "personal" versus "location-related" arrangements? If not, how can we ensure that the institutions are able and equipped to apply a multiple legal framework? It is not for nothing that we see a plea from the field to bring together or harmonize the laws as much as possible, but at the very least this principle must be clear. So please give a more detailed explanation from the government: is that personal aspect of the law really feasible and feasible? A second point that raises questions is the embedding of these laws into effective policies. Forced care is sometimes inevitable, but often comes into the picture where caregivers are powerless to find other solutions. This also has to do with capacity shortages, for example, or with the inability of professionals. Reducing forced care should therefore be the aim of policy and legislation. This means that every effort must be made to ensure adequate and well-trained staff, capable of giving warm human attention to these vulnerable people in an environment so that the compulsion can be minimized, because then there is less need for coercive medication, isolation and bonding. That is why we ask the government if it can give us more insight into the relationship between quality and capacity on the one hand and the need for coercive measures on the other. How has this relationship been investigated so far and how is it involved in the evaluation? Does the government have a target level for minimising coercive measures and what does it do with the field parties to achieve this? What measures are being taken to put the absorption capacity, transfer options and treatment options in place under these three different legal regimes? In particular, how are psychogeriatric care and care for the mentally handicapped, where it is mainly caregivers who also carry out coercive measures, ensure quality, care and professionalism? I'd like an explanation. If coercive measures are needed to provide good care, then the considerations must be made as pure as possible and as practical as possible. That too has been said by colleagues. There is tension between that, because there is a huge spectrum of measures, running from, for example, keeping an outside door locked to forced medication. Where exactly is the border between forced care and common sense in the care of those who cannot fully assess what is good for them? My group shares the principle that any restriction of people's freedom requires care and, if it becomes more specific, the possibility of complaint and appeal. Therefore, the starting point of a care plan with clear frameworks is a good choice in advance. But it cannot be the intention that everything should be boarded up. If a caretaker in psychogeriae brings a wild moving or screaming resident from the living room to his own room because otherwise all residents will be stressed, shouldn't she first go through a step-by-step plan or call a doctor? Or is it? My daughter-in-law, who works in that sector, tells me about it. And outside the institution it is even more complicated, for example in the care of addicts or confused persons, where a form of interference is sometimes essential and one must also be able to act on things. And that is why our question to the government: how are you going to monitor and evaluate the balance between legal protection and feasibility? How do you ensure that caregivers can do their job optimally, without too much fuss, while at the same time ensuring that the rights of vulnerable clients are optimally protected? Will we indeed be able to discuss with each other in the evaluation in a few years' time whether we have perhaps over-estimated it and whether it could perhaps be done with a simpler structure? Within the Care and Force Act, for example, the care plan and the roadmap have been extensively identified, but thanks to all the changes it is now so that when the client or his representative disagrees with the forced care that is always the case external advice should be requested. But that's by definition the case with forced care, isn't it? That means that external advice is always needed. The Minister has already announced that he wants to limit this, because it is not proportionate in this way. The external expert shall only be involved if this is necessary for the proper provision of care. Can the Minister put that more precisely? We know that there is still a legislative amendment to this, but we would like to know which way he is thinking, because it has a direct impact on whether this law puts it all too tight. But apart from that scope: how is this external advice organised? How does the government prevent an army of desks and medical advisers from diving in here to produce all these external advices against big money? Have agreements been made on this subject with the field, so that institutions, for example, can carry out this external consultative task over and over again and thus keep it all a little under control? Next, I have a question about the different responsibilities. Article 2.3 provides for this to ensure the independence of the medical director. Has the Minister considered that, in accordance with the recommendations of the ZonMw research, the medical director should be appointed and supervised by the Supervisory Board rather than directly by the board of the institution? What did that consideration do? As far as the Wzd is concerned, the Minister intends to extend the function of the Wzd doctor to the EZ psychologist or the academicly trained orthopedagogue. In this way, the Minister's amendment to the Leijten amendment is substantially modified. As a reason, he argues to...... at least between the rules of his letter of 18 December by..... the limited capacity and, above all, the lack of added value if there are no medical dimensions involved. Now this comes back as a separate legislative proposal, but the question is whether these dimensions are so simple to distinguish. Many clients with learning difficulties have many visible and invisible physical conditions and risks, so it is a question of whether a behavioral expert has sufficient knowledge to do so. In the case of fixation, for example, you should know that people with intellectual disabilities are at greater risk of osteoporosis. Does an orthopedagogic know that? What is the Minister's view of this interconnectedness of dimensions and what does that mean for the allocation of powers? And then I come to one final question about the responsibilities. With the Wzd and the Wvggz, the outpatient context is rightly given a lot of attention. But that also raises questions. Carers are excluded from the law and are therefore allowed to lock up a demented older or a resident family member with a mental disorder, while an outpatient care worker is not allowed to do so. So it is attractive for that care worker to have the freedom restriction carried out by the carers. How, so my group wants to know from the government, can all kinds of indirect and direct forms of coercion be controlled outside an institutional context? In this context, what are adequate forms of supervision? And then who's the caretaker? That will probably be the GP in many situations. Or are we wrong about that? And if it's the GP, is it equipped to apply these laws? Or should home care organisations, which have to work with coercive measures in the context of Wzd or Wvggz care, hire a doctor to do so? Mr. Chairman. It is clear that we have a positive basic position on these legislative proposals, but that we also see difficulties and eyes, which are directly the result of the fundamental dilemmas that are linked to forced care. To our questions about demarcation and overlap, personal or location-related, capacity and professionalism, legal protection and feasibility, responsibilities and supervision, I would like to summarize them. My colleague, Mr Tineke Strik, will deal with the other aspects.

However, the added value of the protocol is that this individual right of complaint allows clients to invoke the Convention themselves and to appeal to the government for compliance. Mr. Chairman. In many cases, both the Health and Force Act and the Wvggz do not allow an appeal against a judge's decision. This is sometimes the case in cases where it is most necessary, for example in the case of crisis placements. In doing so, files are being built up at a rapid rate and sometimes information does not come out until later, which one would then like to use in a follow-up procedure. From the point of view of strengthening the patient's position, the government initially considered it undesirable to exclude the possibility of appeal. In particular, because of the limitation of freedom rights, the impairment of physical integrity and the nature and duration of the care authorisation, the government saw no reason to withhold the appeal. However, following the request of the Council of State to provide further justification and to answer some of these questions, the government suddenly decided to remove the possibility of appeal. Now the government also indicates that there has not been any need for appeal, without explaining whether this has been investigated and what consequences the deletion could have. As a result of the deletion, the client can no longer appeal against the content of the judge's decision, but only have a higher court assessed by means of a cassation whether a proper procedure has been followed. The problem with this is that this bill shows the judge a lot of discretion in whether or not to hear witnesses or in ordering that investigations should be carried out by experts. There will probably be very few cases in which the Supreme Court will consider that the rights have made a procedural mistake. In our view, it is much more important that the content of the assessment is subject to a judicial review. I'd like a response to that. A further point on legal aid. A prerequisite for the proper legal status of patients is access to high-quality legal assistance. The Van der Meer Committee's report showed that the lawyer's workload has also increased due to a reduction in beds in psychiatric care and the tendency to send people home faster. This would result in more conditional authorisations being imposed. According to the Van der Meer Committee, lawyers indicate that the remuneration of four points for the conditional authorisation is not sufficient, because they are working on it for at least five to seven hours. In the coalition agreement, the cabinet promises to take over the recommendation of the Van der Meer Committee. What's this gonna mean for the flat-rate fee? My group believes that this is an urgent issue, also because, because of the workload, non-specialized lawyers often already represent the interests of very vulnerable clients. Mr. Chairman. As my colleague Mr Ganzevoort has pointed out, with attention and good training, many patients can be persuaded of the importance of appropriate care, so that coercion can be kept to a minimum. Can the Minister promise that the quality/capacity ratio and the need for coercion will be part of the evaluation? Something has already been said about the legislative change that is coming. Perhaps this change in the law can take into account the great dynamics in the names of the Ministry of Justice and Security, so that that name is in line. I do not know whether the slow pace of the change in the law is linked to the dynamics of the names, as this requires constant changes in the law. Mr. Chairman. From the field and from evaluations, the opinion comes out to create a single coherent legal regime, in which different regimes can be included with milder and heavier forms of coercion. This would make the regulation clearer, more accessible and more consistent. My group has the impression that, for the sake of time in particular, the government has opted for two laws, and that is why it has been carrying out an evaluation in two years' time, as a kind of compromise. My group understands the urgency and urgency, but also sees the disadvantages and risks of a radical change in the system, because perhaps a few years later this will go on again if it becomes apparent that a coherent legal system is actually preferable. Fast-tracking fundamental changes can be disastrous both for the field and for patients. How do we prevent them from being burdened with enormous challenges for implementation and adaptation, when we may be going back to doing so a few years later? I'd like an answer to that. Thank you.

Then external expertise makes sense. The way things stand in the bill, hence my letter, is that any decision to impose coercion as part of the healthcare plan should involve an external expert. That seems to me to be a real job, and that is a waste of time, because we are far too busy in the care sector. It is not necessary, because multidisciplinary decisions can also be made within an institution. That does not always require an external expert. It was asked: is that another army of people who are going to eat from it very well? I hope not, too. The suggestion you make is extremely practical and right, namely: let it be someone from another institution. So yes, external expertise and a critical, independent four-eyed look from the outside, but please not another separate profession. That won't be necessary at all. You can also invoke the external expertise of fellow institutions. In fact, that is actually better, because they have more experience in the same setting. I am very happy with that figure, because I think it has made the law better. Mr Schnabel asked how I value that. I think it's made the law better. Greater equality in legal protection has been achieved between the GGZ target group and the FG and PG target group. I think this is an added value. In addition, an independent look has been organised. That too, I think, is an added value. But I also see a practical objection, which the field has strongly pointed out. In disability care, for example, there is often no relationship between the client and a doctor at all, because there is no medical problem at all. Also the coercive measures will have no medical background at all but above all a educational background. The question is whether it is possible to appoint someone from another profession, for example someone who is already in charge of the final responsibility for care in that institution and who is a social welfare psychologist. Can it be pointed out in the position of Wzd-doctor? It's a bit like the medical director, but then copied to the other sectors. I think that would be a very good solution, because it improves the content. But, and I hope I may say that before there is any interruption, I would like it to be someone who is subject to disciplinary law. I also think that this has been the background to the appointment of that Wzd doctor in the House of Representatives. It is not only a question of ensuring independence but also of ensuring that someone is actually subject to disciplinary law. That's why I would think: a gz psychologist is BIG registered and therefore subject to disciplinary law. An orthopedagogic eye is not just that, because it is governed by private law and not by public law. So I would say that an orthopedagogic eye can, but only if it has actually been added to the BIG by 1 January 2020. That is the intention, but if that is not feasible then we should not do it for a while.

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u122	Thank you for the announcement.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u123	Mr. Chairman. I am glad that we are here today dealing with the law on compulsory mental health care in connection with the Law on care and coercion and the Law on forensic care. I will try to answer the questions of the Chamber in three blocks. First, let me give you a general introduction. Then I come to the content of the bill. I would like to dwell on the implementation and, finally, on a number of other questions. The purpose of the Law is to provide better appropriate care, appropriate to both the nature of the care and the time at which it takes place. The compulsory ggz Act lays down the criteria, principles and procedure for the application of care to persons with serious mental problems and, as a result, serious harm to themselves or to others. It is important that compulsory care is always an ultimatum remedium: only if there is no other way. I think all the spokesmen have rightly pointed this out. Early warning should attempt to use timely care to reduce problems and prevent mandatory care. Care at local level is of great importance. Several spokesmen have also pointed this out, and I will go into it further later. In this context, Mr Schnabel has rightly referred to interference as an important phenomenon. Others, I think, used that term as well. If compulsory care is needed, then as short as possible. For clients with mental impairment, with as a result a risk of serious harm to themselves or their environment, the current BOPZ Act is limited, too limited in its possibilities and does not fit in with the outpatientisation of care. The current BOPZ Act focuses on the forced admission of the client and not the treatment. With the law mandatory ggz this changes. This law focuses on the treatment of the client and no longer on the place where he gets care. The link between forced admission and treatment is loosened in the law on compulsory ggz. That makes this law personal instead of locational. Forced admission is not the starting point but only one of the forms of compulsory care, the starting point being that the care must be provided as much as possible in an outpatient manner. The law on compulsory ggz, as well as the law on care and compulsion, has considerably more possibilities to offer customization. This is desirable because the need for care, the severity of the disadvantage and the required level of security of the client may vary over time. Therefore, this bill allows for a multitude of forms of care and security, in which the form of care, the degree of compulsion and the level of security can be tailored to the specific care needs of the client at that time. I deliberately choose my wording in order care, degree of compulsion, level of security. The law mandatory ggz is not a security law. Security may be a condition for providing good care, but it's about care. We can therefore conclude that the Law on compulsory ggz offers more treatment options than the BOPZ Act. In addition, the mandatory ggz Act also strengthens the legal status of the client. This will give the client the opportunity to draw up a plan of action to prevent mandatory care. It is laid down by law that his preference should be taken into account in the case of compulsory care and all forms of forced care should be subject to prior judicial review. All shapes. In the current legislation, only the compulsory pre-inclusion is subject to judicial review. Not only the position of the client is strengthened, also the position of the family gets more attention. All the spokesmen have rightly pointed this out. That's important because family members are often the first to see that someone isn't doing well. For example, the family is now given the legal opportunity to give an opinion on the care plan. Mr Schnabel asked an interesting and interesting question about this: how do you weigh the importance between the family confidant on the one hand and the patient confidant on the other? In response, I would like to say that the patient confidant is there for the patient, as the word suggests. He should assist them throughout the process of compulsory care. This concerns both the preparation, implementation and termination of compulsory care. The family confidant is precisely there for the family and neighbors relevant to the continuity of care. This helps the family by, for example, informing, advising and assisting them in complaint procedures. Both have compulsory ggz free access to the data subject without the consent of others, but the family confidant can only speak to the data subject if the data subject expressly agrees. I think Mr Schnabel has a question, Mr President.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u124	I noticed that. I give the floor to Mr Schnabel.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u125	My point was this. When we look at the role of the patient confidant, according to the regulations in that area and the practice of almost 30 years now, the patient confidant is standing next to the patient and whatever the patient wants, even if it actually seems very unreasonable, also in the eyes of the patient confidant. That could be very contrary to what the family wants. Even if the family doesn't do it out of self-interest, but with the idea "that's not good at all for our son or our daughter, or our father or mother, that this or that happens, that he wants it." Both have access to the same subject matter, both have access to the patient, both have access to the pieces that it is about, but they are in a very different position. ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u146	That is a fair question and the answer is yes. Mr. Chairman. I shall deal with harmonization. As I have just said, the compulsory ggz Act regulates the criteria, the principles and the procedure for how people with serious mental problems, with as a result serious harm to themselves or to others, can be treated against their will. At the request of the First Chamber, the legislative proposals are even more aligned and harmonised as far as possible. The recommendations of ZonMw, which carried out a thematic legislative review towards forced care, have been used. I would like to mention four examples of this harmonisation in my contribution. Firstly, in the legislative proposals, the key concepts are harmonised as much as possible, as well as the criterion of forced care. For example, in all three legislative proposals, the concept of "care" is now defined from the point of view of content and not from the point of view of funding, as was originally the case with two of the three legislative proposals, the Wzd and the Wfz. Secondly, the criterion for forced care has been harmonised. Both in the Wzd and in the compulsory ggz Act, the criterion is that there must be a risk of serious disadvantage for the client or for others. Many spokesmen have asked questions about this concept, very intrusive by Mr Don and Mrs Barth. Mrs Barth asked whether the Secretary of State could explain how the case law has evolved from the "danger" criterion to "serious disadvantage" that these two terms now essentially mean the same thing. I think that we should broaden the scope of this question because several Members of your Chamber have asked questions about the term'serious disadvantage'. I would like to emphasise in my contribution that this term does not mean a material extension to the original legislative proposal, nor to the hazard criterion under the BOPZ. Harmonisation of this term has taken place to emphasise that the criteria for imposing forced care on the basis of both legislative proposals are the same. The elements which constitute the serious disadvantage of the criterion are almost the same as those of the hazard criterion under the BOPZ Act.
ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u147	Maybe I'm getting ahead of what the Secretary of State wants to say, but I'm still trying to get it sharp. I call it serious disadvantage versus the hazard criterion. In my view, the hazard criterion is really linked to the BOPZ Act. That law is a recording law and not a health bill. Danger means you have to act on the basis of a psychiatric disorder through a rapture. If you want to apply further measures, you have to take sanctions. At severe disadvantage you talk much more about the personal situation of someone who, due to a disorder, is a disadvantage to themselves, but there is no connection to a recording. There is a possibility of compulsory care, although it is also a recording, but you can at least let go of a number of stages. A serious disadvantage in the home situation can mean something other than the hazard criterion from the BOPZ. In my view, this is not a way of aligning the concepts.

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u155	I get the point of both of you. I hope I can give you an answer that will help you, even if there's no lawyer here before you. Mr Don asked how early action under the compulsory ggz Act is possible if the criteria remain the same. In response, I say that it is true that the criteria for the provision of forced care are the same. There must be a serious disadvantage that is likely to be caused by a psychiatric condition. This law is a treatment law and puts the treatment at the centre, at the place that is best for the patient, even if it is in an outpatient setting. I come to the procedure and the actors and to the content of the law. Many actors are involved in the imposition and implementation of mandatory care. An application must be prepared before a court. The Public Prosecutor has an important role to play in preparing it. He's got the applicant's role. The Public Prosecutor has been given this role in the law following the recommendations of the Hoekstra Commission, which examined the facts and circumstances of Els Borst's tragic death. The committee's recommendations suggested that the Public Prosecutor should be the person to perform this applicant's role, because compulsory care is at the border of criminal law, especially where there is forced admission and thus deprivation of liberty. Mr Van Dijk asked: are there now sufficient instruments to be able to intervene effectively if there is not enough threat of manifestation of immediate serious disadvantage? The answer is as follows. Indeed, the failure to include the previously proposed observation measure creates a gap. We can't deny that. However, this can be limited by using a broader explanation of the concept of "immediately" in the context of the crisis measure in relation to the threat of serious harm. In fact, a meeting with practitioners has shown that the term "immediately" needs to be more open and can be filled in because of the very different situations that need to be assessed. There was agreement that the term should be used in the context of the specific case. The term "immediately" does not necessarily mean that it is expected that the serious disadvantage will manifest itself within a given period, for example 24 hours. That is why the legislative proposal does not include a specific time limit linked to the term "immediately" and an open standard has been chosen as a directive. In addition, the Act provides mandatory ggz the possibility to provide a person with temporary compulsory care for up to 18 hours prior to taking a crisis measure if it can reasonably be assumed that a crisis measure should be taken. That period can also be used for further investigation, if one reasonably suspects that a crisis measure is necessary, but is not quite sure yet. In this case, a person may be transferred to an appropriate research area for the purpose of making a better diagnosis. In this respect, there is the restriction that the process of a crisis measure must be very clear, or that there is a presumption that this will be necessary. All this in response to Mr Van Dijk's question. With regard to the observation measure, or more generally, he has asked another question: is the gap created by it further filled so that an unsafe society is prevented? Mrs Bikker also asked questions about this. She asked by whom a person should be heard when imposing a crisis measure. It also asked what is meant by the term "if possible." The answer to that is that the obligation rests with the mayor anyway. He can mandate that, even to someone outside the city. The term "if possible" refers to the situation that the person concerned does not want to be heard or cannot be contacted. There are different variants of that. However, the mayor must not assume too lightly that someone does not want to be heard or cannot be heard. He'll have to make an effort to do that. If I value it correctly, that's a tooth heavier than what's happening under the BOPZ Act. If the hearing doesn't happen, the mayor should be able to motivate why not. An important difference from the retention under the BOPZ Act is that mandatory care can now be applied prior to the crisis measure if there is a reasonable presumption that a crisis measure will be taken. Therefore, the provision of appropriate care should not be delayed until the measure has been issued. If mandatory care has been imposed by the judge or mayor, the person responsible for the care must assess the effectiveness, proportionality and safety of its implementation in the specific situation. Mr Ganzevoort asked if I could clarify when which law applies, especially when there are combinations of aspects in an individual, for example a combination of detention and an LVB or detention and ggz. The answer is: forensic care can only be imposed if there is a criminal offence. So that regime is very clear. When forensic care is not at issue, the question of which problem is at the forefront of what appropriate care is and thus, as a derivative, what law applies, the Wzd or the compulsory ggz Act. The professionals involved make the assessment of which problem is dominant. In this context, Mr Don has asked a question about the difference between the will of people with and without mental illness. Both legislative proposals are based on willpower. Willingness can fluctuate in time or be related to certain decisions. Someone, for example, cannot be willing to decide on money matters but can be willing to take care of them. Within an individual there can be gradations. Following the recommendations of the Thematic Law Review, it has been stipulated that willful resistance is met if there is serious harm to the individual alone. This means that in such a case, as a specific answer to the question, he can choose not to be treated. That's no different than someone with a somatic condition like cancer. When in the case of willful resistance there is also a serious disadvantage for another person, it is of course different. It is then possible to intervene, whereby the will of the person concerned can be overruled. The same applies if there is an acute risk of life for the person concerned. This test question is always asked: is there suicidal behaviour? On the basis of that test, a decision is made.

Mr. Chairman. Questions have been asked, among other things, by Mr Van Dijk about the sharing of information in the Security House. Safety houses are partnerships for tackling cross-chain issues. The Security House has no legal function of its own. Therefore, there is no legal basis for sharing data in this context. Participating parties shall retain their own responsibilities and powers. Each participating professional must always consider what information he may share with other participants within his powers. In order to support this assessment, the National Steering Group Security Houses has established the handle "Data sharing in the care and security domain." This is an important document, because it contains what is allowed under the applicable privacy regulations. For a care professional, the outcome of the assessment may be that medical professional secrecy prevents the sharing of patient data, but in some cases information other than the information covered by that confidentiality obligation is sufficient to allow the person concerned to be appropriately treated in the event of contact by the police with the person concerned. The Security House can also contribute to the information position of the police while respecting privacy and medical confidentiality. Mr. Chairman. I'm gonna get back to the issue of outpatient care. The law mandatory ggz and the law care and coercion are in line with the changing social beliefs that care should take place as much as possible in the client's own environment and that patients who are able to do so should be able to lead as much as possible an integrated life in society. The current BOPZ Act is not sufficient in this respect. The Health and Force Act and the Law mandatory ggz are innovative in this respect. These laws make it possible under strict conditions that forced care can also be provided outside classical institutions. People in urgent need of care can stay at home with forced care, but they can also be accommodated in small-scale housing if this leads to unsafe situations. I just turned that in. This can happen, for example, in a form of sheltered housing. Of course, care must always be responsible and safe. It is therefore also expressly not the case that care should now be given out of work. Ambulant compulsion offers the opportunity to offer more customization. The requirements for outpatient care are already largely laid down in the legislative proposals themselves. Those terms are known. The requirements of ultimum remedium, proportionality, subsidiarity and security can be considered. The procedure set out in the legislative proposals is also fully applicable to outpatient care. The general measures of governance under the compulsory ggz and the Wzd Act specify the requirements under which care can be provided in the outpatient sector. These general measures of governance are currently for consultation in the field. Discussions conducted with the field in spring 2017 have shown that care in the outpatient situation needs to be flexible and meet the specific care needs of the client, taking into account its preferences. In the AMvBs, we have translated this into a way that includes the need to regulate a number of issues, including the accessibility of the care provider and the supervision, but that there is scope to take account of the specific situation when determining how this is done. The consultation period is due to expire on 4 February. I'm very curious about the input of the field. Questions have been asked about this too. Mr Schnabel asked if there were any answers to the questions surrounding the consultation and whether the directors could indicate whether and to what extent the directives are already being experimentally worked on and what the results are. Participation is still ongoing, but it is well known that, for example, GGZ is in favour of the Netherlands or does not wish to rule out fixation in advance in the outpatient situation. This is an important input point of GGZ Nederland. Well, we're gonna have to deal with that. Another point is about experimentation, also a question from Mr Schnabel. At the moment, it simply cannot be experimented because there is no legal basis. You can come up with all kinds of pilots, but you can't experiment with outpatient coercion because it's not legal yet. Mrs Baay asked why we regulate so much through the AMvB and not in the law. A lot of important things are regulated in the laws. I've already mentioned a lot of them. The additional guarantees that will be provided in both AMvBs stem in particular from the difference between the setting within an accommodation and the setting outside an accommodation. That's what the AMvBs are all about. And as I pointed out: on February 4th, the consultation of the people who know about it in the country. Mrs De Bruijn asked, in the context of an out-of-arm force, whether the Secretary of State was not concerned that due to the lack of sufficient professional supervision, compulsory admission would still be decided upon. I will come back later on to the extent to which olefinisation is taking shape, but of course the starting point is that care must always be responsible and safe. Of course it is not the case that care should now be provided in an outpatient manner. It's the starting point. In fact, we want to give as little care as possible. Lack of staff or supervision should not be a reason to take someone in instead of treating them out of work if that is better. Therefore, the AMvB leaves the assessment of supervision as far as possible to the healthcare provider and, for example, it is not required that two healthcare providers are present at all times. Capacity is also being worked on. Field parties are working in the framework of the waiting lists to increase capacity. I shall come back to that very briefly. Mrs De Bruijn also asked about the possibilities for compulsory care in the outpatient situation of the Secretary of State. As an option for compulsory care at home, I see, among other things, mandatory medication. At the moment it is not yet possible to administer compulsory medication at home. However, under the BOPZ Act, there is the possibility of a conditional authorisation, which makes it necessary to provide for outpatient treatment as a condition to prevent forced admission. If a patient does not comply with that condition and refuses to receive ambulatory treatment, he must be admitted to treatment against his will, because that is the only alternative. The law on compulsory ggz does not require this in the case of the person in question who wants to have an outpatient treatment at home, e.g. a compulsory medication, although this is part of the healthcare authorisation. In such a situation, he can also be transferred to a clinic, for example an outpatient clinic, to be treated. After that, the person concerned can return home. By not letting the person in question have a choice, you want to work in front of them. Thus, the mandatory ggz Act is based on a fundamentally different principle than the BOPZ Act. A conditional authorisation under the BOPZ Act places the compulsory admission at the centre of intervention, while the compulsory ggz Act we are talking about puts the treatment at the heart of the process.

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u217	Mr. Chairman. I too would like to express my thanks for the input of all Members. You could say: all good comes in threes.
