I would like to concentrate on the Forensic Care Act, which is different in character from the other two legislative proposals. It is not so much a legal status law but much more a key case and organization law, with some legal position elements. I think it's also a pleasure to be able to deal with that law here. Mrs Oomen already referred to it: the reason for this bill is in this Chamber, with a motion by a former member of your Chamber, Mr Van de Beeten. By the way, that motion dates back more than 14 years. It's been a long time. To be precise, it was July 2004. That motion was a response to a number of problems in practice as a result of the fact that the regular care system did not actually fit in with the care needed in a criminal context. For example, it was found in the prison system that there was an increasing number of prisoners with psychiatric problems who could not be given the necessary care. It was necessary to reduce the risk of relapse and resocialization. In addition, it was often difficult for the parole board to realize appropriate treatment options for individuals with care as a special condition, as an additional measure. One of the reasons for this was the fact that funding was provided through health insurance and that there were waiting lists in the care. The Justice Department had no way of directing itself on care in a criminal context. As a result, the Minister of Justice was unable to fulfil his responsibility for the proper enforcement of penalties. This bill gives the Minister of JenV more opportunities to steer forensic care. In the end, that is very important if you want to do something about the reduction of relapse and, through a more personalised approach, to ensure that those in need of care end up in the right place. In the direction of Mr Dercksen, I would say directly: on tbs, an important part of forensic care, you can, of course, be critical, but the recidivism rates for people returning to society from the TBs are still significantly lower than for prisoners returning to society from the regular prison system. At TBs you are talking about 19%, while that percentage is many times higher in the regular prison system, at about 47%. I deal with the questions along a number of themes. I want to talk about the continuity of care in relation to Article 2.3 of the Forensic Care Act. I would like to discuss a number of organisational aspects of the law. Think of powers, indication and placement, and procurement. I should like to refer to the interpretation by the prosecutor of the applicant's role, which may, moreover, be brief and the reasons why it has been chosen. Colleagues have already said something about it. I can tell you a little bit more about the way in which this is being implemented later. I also have a block of varia, but I will start with the main theme, on which most of the questions have been asked in this Chamber, but which also gets a lot of attention outside this Chamber: the issue of the refusing observers. Mr. Chairman. As you know, the NIFP reports on the presence of a mental disorder in suspects of serious crimes. The Pieter Baan Centre reports annually on about 200 observandi. In recent years, it has not been a very recent increase; we see that this increase has taken place a number of years earlier; we see that this leads to about half of those 200 observers refusing to participate in that study. It's about 100 suspects a year, even though we see at the same time that the average treatment time is decreasing. I refer to Mrs Barth's question on this subject. It's not like with a shorter treatment period more suspects are involved in the investigation now. That is a problem in my view, because if suspects do not cooperate, it will be very difficult for psychiatrists to come up with good and conclusive advice on the presence of a disorder at the time of the crime and thus answer the question of whether there is a need and desirability to impose a TBS measure. By refusing to cooperate, suspects try to avoid a TBS measure. As you know, Mr Schnabel referred to it, as did Mrs Barth O'Honnell, that a concluding opinion of the NIFP is not necessarily a requirement for the imposition of a TBS measure. That is also settled case law of the Supreme Court. However, in cases where this advice is not available, the judge must be able to reach a judgment that there is a disorder on other grounds. That is actually the key point for the courts to decide on the imposition of TBs. If this is not done, then there is a very undesirable and potentially dangerous situation. Perpetrators, perpetrators of serious crimes, will not receive the treatment they need. This is bad for themselves, but it also brings with it a danger to society. There may be casualties. That can be prevented if someone is being treated properly for his disorder. In order to do something about this, the Law on Forensic Care includes the so-called "Registering Observandi." Of course, there has been a lot of discussion about this in recent years. I also think that the call for and urgency of such an arrangement has also recently become topical again as a result of the terrible murder of Anne Faber. Mr. Chairman. The same scheme allows, as a last resort, as an ultimatum remedium, in the case of serious crimes for the purposes of the pro-justitia reports, to obtain existing medical data on non-observators without obtaining their consent from their traders. Of course, that is a far-reaching proposal. Breaking something essential such as medical professional secrecy and the right to change for medical professionals is a fundamental principle in our system. This also means that we have to deal with this extremely reluctantly. I understand very well that the Chamber is asking critical questions about this. I think we did the same here behind the table. But if you look at the bill, especially the modifications that have taken place, you will see that we have tried to keep that breach as limited as possible and to provide some very specific safeguards in the law. Before I go into some of the questions you have raised about necessity, proportionality and subsidiarity, I would like to briefly mention some of the safeguards in the law. This concerns both procedural and legal safeguards, while preserving the confidentiality of the data to be obtained as far as possible. For example, the circle of persons to whom the data are provided has been kept as small as possible. Only the members of the multidisciplinary committee and, in the event of a legal authorisation being issued, the rapporteurs will eventually have access to that information. You could say: they're medical records. This means, for example, that the data does not go to the Public Prosecutor's Office; the Public Prosecutor does not have access to it. I think it is important to make this very clear. It's really about being a committee, where doctors are part of, providing good advice. Another important guarantee is located in the independent committee that assesses whether medical data is available and advises on its usability. An independent psychiatrist will chair that committee and the doctors and behavioral experts who are on that committee have already been kept confidential by virtue of their profession, but, in addition to the functional confidentiality obligations of these members, it is stipulated for the entire committee that it is subject to confidentiality. In that connection, Mrs De Bruijn asked how exactly you are going to maintain that. Let me say first of all that I assume that it is not necessary to maintain this, but in the event that this is necessary and that the obligation of confidentiality is violated in that committee, Article 272 of the criminal law, which deliberately violates the secret that a person is obliged to keep under his office, profession or legal order, is punishable. Moreover, there is, of course, also a specific disciplinary procedure for BIG registered persons. I would just like to briefly mention the whole list of guarantees for short-term reasons. If there's any questions about it, you're gonna have to interrupt. These are the two safeguards I have just mentioned, but also, for example, the fact that it is possible to break through only in the case of very serious crimes, not in all cases, but where there really is a crime of life or serious sex crimes, that there is an ultimatum remedium and that there is a target bond. I say this in the direction of Mrs Bikker and also Mrs Barth, who asked questions about this. It is therefore not the case that these data may be used in criminal proceedings. It is really just a question of getting a good opinion: is there a disorder and is it desirable and necessary for TBs to be imposed? I also mention the safeguards regarding the destruction of data, the double judicial review and the suspensive effect during an appeal procedure, i.e. in cassation and appeal, the involvement of suspects and the information position of suspects. That's all been thought of. In other words, there are a lot of safeguards in this legislative proposal which, in my view, lead to an infringement of something fundamental, but which is surrounded by a lot of safeguards. Now I come to the point where several Members challenged me: what is actually the need and why exactly should this be? I saw with Mrs Bikker but also with Mrs Barth and Mrs Strik that they have doubts or are not convinced yet. I'm gonna do everything I can to convince them anyway. As I have just said, a legal breach of medical professional secrecy must be used with caution, but at the same time professional secrecy is not absolute. It also follows from the case law, for example of the European Court of Human Rights, that such a breach is permitted where there is an overriding requirement in the public interest. I am convinced that this situation is happening here, because we are talking about suspects of very serious crimes directed against life or serious sex crimes, where there is a very high risk of recurrence if the perpetrators are not treated for their disorder. That also has huge social consequences. These crimes intervene deeply in the lives of victims, but they also affect the sense of social security. In the case of suspects of this type of crime, it is necessary to determine whether there is a disorder in the context of the imposition of penalties. If that is the case, then it is important that we are able to impose the most appropriate sanction, with the right place for care if there is a disorder.

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u266	Second term. I'd be fine with that. Mr. Chairman. Then there is the other major issue that we were discussing today. That is improving the continuity of care. It is important that care does not stop automatically when the criminal record ends. An important means of promoting the connection of the regular care is the competence of the criminal court to issue, at each stage of the journey or subsequent to the criminal record, a care authorisation under the Law mandatory ggz or a withdrawal authorisation under the Wzd. This is regulated by the so-called liaison Article 2.3. The district attorney can make a request for this, but the judge can also decide of his own motion and therefore does not have to wait, I say in the direction of Mrs Strik. For that issue it is required that the criteria of compulsory care of the Law mandatory ggz and of the Law care and coercion are met. The criminal justice officer also follows the procedures laid down in the Law on Health and Enforcement and those procedures have been further developed in the course of the laws, as the Secretary of State of VWS has just mentioned, and thus the coherence between the three legislative proposals has been strengthened. Mr Dercksen, Mrs Bikker and Mrs Strik pointed out that representatives of the Council for Justice and the Public Prosecutor were somewhat critical at the hearing. I regret that in itself. I do not think it is right either, but sometimes this is linked to a number of fundamental considerations that we have made with regard to these legislative proposals. In my view, Mrs Barth challenged me to do so, of sticking to Article 2.3 and to the regimes that it sees fit to do so. My answer to that is yes. On the basis of the comments made by Mr Dercksen and Mrs Strik, I would first of all like to point out that it has always been the intention of the legislator to apply similar criteria to the compulsory admission under the BOPZ Act and the criminal placement in a psychiatric hospital, the BOPZ criminal law. The Public Prosecutor considers that a strict review of the criteria of the Law would create a gap because, in the current situation, it would also prevent insanity-based suspects who would voluntarily wish to be placed in the care now under Article 37 Criminal law being forced into a psychiatric hospital. This would no longer be possible under the new legislation, because forced care can only be dealt with once all the voluntary possibilities have been exhausted. There are a number of comments to be made. First of all, I would like to stress that the remarks made by the prosecution do not concern people who have committed a tbs-worthy fact. In that case, tbs may be imposed. Tbs is also possible when someone is completely insane. These are therefore insanity suspects who have committed acts that are not so serious as to be able to impose TBs and who indicate their willingness to be given voluntary treatment. I do not think that the word 'empty' is entirely appropriate in this context, if you look at the small group of suspects that this would relate to in the future. That forced care cannot be dealt with until all the voluntary alternatives have been exhausted is a very fundamental starting point for the legislative proposals we are discussing from the outset. If we agree on that point of view, I do not see why this principle should suddenly cease to apply when it is the criminal court that imposes the authorisation of care rather than the civil court. Then I come to the Council's criticism of the case law, which focuses on the procedure of the Law mandatory ggz and the Health and Force Act. This is a rule that requires the criminal court to apply civil procedural law. With regard to the fear that this is not workable, I think it will be very difficult in practice. We attach great importance to the uniformity of the procedure. Where possible, it is associated with the regular procedures of both laws, with a view to harmonising procedures, equivalent legal protection and the legal unit. We want to avoid, as in the case of, for example, the criminal BOPZ measure under Article 37, that different criteria are also applied as a result of different procedures and would result in legal inequality. This is all the more true now that the 2.3 authorisation is regular care and will no longer be a criminal measure. In other words, we believe that, and this is how I answer Mrs Barth's question on this point, the procedure as it stands is well designed and, in our view, is also workable. I do not share the Council's fear of the case law, which Mr Dercksen, Mrs Bikker and Mrs Wezel are calling for attention to be given to, namely that, as a result of the possibility of cassation under the Law, compulsory ggz problems arise because that possibility is not available at tbs. We assume that in the event that a TBs still fulfils the condition of extension and TBs is still the appropriate framework for him, the court will also extend the TBs measure. In cases where he considers that to be justified, he may also choose to terminate the compulsory care on a conditional basis. So he can impose different conditions on that. If a person no longer fulfils the conditions for the extension of the TBs, or if TBs are considered to be too heavy a framework, the TBs extension judge may now choose to issue a care authorisation of his own. This care authorisation is also feasible in case of stock and must be implemented within two weeks. Setting cassation has no suspensive effect. All in all, I think that criminals should be able to quickly acquire this new jurisdiction, which is a little more in the civil sphere. Mr Dercksen proposed that Article 37 of the Code of Criminal Law be retained for a while. I do not think this is wise, because then you really get two regimes side by side with different legal protection and different legal status. I don't think that's wise. Mrs Strik asked why the defendant in respect of whom the criminal court grants a care authorisation pursuant to Article 2.3 can appeal against the judgment in his criminal proceedings, but not against the refusal of care authorisation. The short answer to this is: because under the Law mandatory ggz also no appeal is open against the authorisation of care. So the legal status of the 2.3 patients is the same as that of the regular ggz patients, you might say. That is actually part of what these laws aim to do, namely harmonisation. Mr Schnabel indicated that he was concerned about the difference in the composition of the patient population with a care authorisation in the ggz.

In particular, because the government itself had given all the arguments in the original proposals as to why it is so important to introduce them.

ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u269	No, Mr President, that was the question of the coincidence and the difference in it. ParlaMint-NL_2018-01-15-eerstekamer-3.u270	If you don't get an answer, I'd like to take it with you in the second term. Mr Schnabel indicated that he was concerned about the difference in the composition of the patient populations with a care authorisation in the ggz. In his view, this could pose a security risk for these institutions. It is not the intention of the Forensic Care Act to place people in the regular ggz who do not belong there or who could cause danger there. The judge has the possibility under Article 2.3 to place people in the regular ggz, but this option is only intended for situations where the person concerned is also better placed in that regular ggz. This has also been taken into account in the indication. This is done, for example, by the NIFP, which can also make a good assessment of what is important in terms of treatment and security, so that this can be explicitly taken into account in an opinion. Mrs Barth asked a number of questions which also very much relate to how this works in practice and which affect the continuity of care in practice. Think, for example, of the connection between forensic care and regular care. I would like to tell Mrs Barth that we have been running a programme with different stakeholders for some time now, you might say, the Continuity of Care programme. It looks very strongly at what is needed to bring a criminal justice framework and regular care closer together. This does not solve all the problems immediately, but Mrs Barth also asked: what happens when the programme ends? There are already agreements between all the partners involved that will also be met periodically at the end of the programme to see what progress has been made on the follow-up agreements. Then one can keep things in the light with one another that can be better. One thing that plays a role in this is the finding from AEF's report that there would be too few suitable places. Quite specifically, AEF has found that there are actually too few secure places in the regular ggz and in mental disability care, which you need for very serious cases. When a care authorisation under Article 2.3 is imposed, such a place may be needed. That is far from the case in all cases. As regards the progress in increasing the supply of secure places, there are also a lot of conversations between regular care. I am also talking about this with my colleague, the Secretary of State of the United States of America, to see if we can come up with so-called field standards, with agreements on numbers of places, the provision of secure care, the necessary capacity and the conditions under which they can be made available. Our ambition will be to have it ready by 1 April. I also had a taste of some concerns with Mrs Barth about the connection of disability care with all this kind of arrangement. I'd like to dive into that one more time. I had the impression that VGN participates well. For example, VGN is also involved in this programme in both the steering group and the working group. My predecessor also concluded a covenant of forensic concern with a lot of improvements and agreements over and over again, where they are one of the covenant partners, and frankly I would like to keep it that way. For example, they are also involved in the development of field standards. But if you ask me specifically to pay particular attention to that, I am happy to do so. The same applies to probation, because, as Mrs Barth also points out, that is an important partner in all of this. Mr. Chairman. Then I would like to turn to a number of organisational aspects, because that is the essence of this organizational law, a law that contains regulations relating to forensic institutions: procurement, financing, indication, data processing, control. A number of questions have been asked about this, and with regard to the TBs institutions, Mr Van Dijk asked whether sufficient measures were being taken to prevent the high turnover of staff. That too is something you of course do not regulate with a law, but several agreements have been made with the forensic care sector. Take, for example, the 2013 Multiannual Covenant or the Quality Programme for Forensic Care, which looks at both quality assurance and what it takes to achieve it, and staff are also incredibly important. I have the impression that the sector is also working very hard to attract and retain sufficiently qualified staff, as well as to train the people who work there. A good staff policy also requires a safe working environment. Of this, one could say that the legislative proposals we are discussing today are intended to contribute to this, for example because, for ggz patients who are in a TBs clinic, a number of management powers of the Principles Law will be made available to them and applied accordingly. So in that sense, this bill will also help. Then Mrs Barth challenged me by suggesting that you should actually choose, if you look at this law: either you stay in the execution of forensic care by pulling it towards you and arranging it administratively, or you do it through some kind of purchasing relationship. Why choose the construct as laid down here, where you rely on private institutions but create far-reaching powers to intervene? This is closely linked to the fact that the implementation of the TBs relates to a very limited number of institutions, where in my view it is really necessary to have a stick behind the door for individual cases where things can really go wrong. After all, this is a serious target group which has committed serious crimes and which is also specifically at stake in the field of social security. So I do not want to get into the situation that I have to go to the Chamber to say that there is an unsafe situation in a TBs clinic, but that I do not really have powers to act against it. That is the background to the powers that have been taken into account in this legislative proposal, which, of course, cannot be applied if you do not like the price negotiated, and there really must be matters going on in the security sphere or in the operational sphere which may pose a danger and which require you to act. Mr Derksen had a question about powers. Can you act now enough if there are any abuses? This kind of power will help if it relates to the total institution. Of course, we often do not want it to come to this. There may be incidents despite all the strict requirements. I can tell you that these incidents are also always part of conversations between the clinic and the Justice Department, and that DJI regularly asks for a so-called internal investigation. How did something happen and what can an institution do to prevent it from happening in the future? If that is not enough, there is also an inspection that can carry out an investigation at any time there.
